CHANGE OF REGULARITY IN CONTROLLABILITY AND OBSERVABILITY OF SYSTEMS OF WAVE EQUATIONS
Thomas Perrin

Abstract




Abstract.

Solutions of a system of wave equations are constructed for both homogeneous and inhomogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions at every regularity level. We prove that boundary observability, and thus boundary exact controllability, at some regularity level is equivalent to boundary observability at all levels. The main ingredient is the ellipticity of a time-derivative on the Neumann trace of the solution, which is proved by microlocal techniques.

Introduction

Let
(M,g)
be a
n
-dimensional compact Riemannian manifold with boundary. We write
\partial M
for its boundary and
\inte M=M\backslash\partial M
. Let
N
be a positive integer. Consider a first-order differential operator
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,TM\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
, acting on functions from
M
to
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, given in a coordinate chart
(U,x)
by

X=X^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}},\quad\text{ with }X^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})\text{ for }j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket.

Consider also
q\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
, and write
\mathsf{P}
for the operator
\mathsf{P}=\Delta-X-q
, where
\Delta=\Delta\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}
is the (vectorial) Laplace-Beltrami operator associated with the metric
g
. Denote by
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
the adjoint of
\mathsf{P}
. It has the same form as
\mathsf{P}
. We will define a family of spaces of Sobolev type, written
\mathcal{K}^{s}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}
, corresponding to compatibility conditions adapted to
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
.

Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
T>0
,
\Theta=\left(\Theta_{1},\cdots,\Theta_{N}\right)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, and set

\diag(\Theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\Theta_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\Theta_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\Theta_{N}\end{pmatrix}.

Solutions of the wave equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

(1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}v-\mathsf{P}^{\ast}v&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(v(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}v(0,\cdot)\right)&=&0&&\text{in }M,\\
v&=&\diag(\Theta)f&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

(2)

are given, for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
and
f\in H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. We write
\partial_{\nu}u=\left(\partial_{\nu}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{C}^{N}}\right)u
for the normal derivative of
u
.

Definition 1

(
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta
)
.

We say that
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta
holds if there exists
C>0
such that for all
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
,

\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}\leq C\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Definition 2

(
H^{s}
-exact controllability for
\Theta
)
.

We say that
H^{s}
-exact controllability for
\Theta
holds if for all
(\varphi^{0},\varphi^{1})\in\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1}
, there exists
f\in H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that

(v(T),\partial_{t}v(T))=(\varphi^{0},\varphi^{1}).

A duality property for solutions of (
1
) and (
2
) implies that the classical controllability - observability equivalence is satisfied.

Lemma 3
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
H^{s}
-exact controllability for
\Theta
and
H^{-s}
-observability for
\Theta
are equivalent.

The main result of this article is the following.

Theorem 4
.

Consider
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. If
s_{1}<s_{2}
then for all
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
H^{s_{1}}
-observability for
\Theta
implies
H^{s_{2}}
-observability for
\Theta
. If
s_{1}>s_{2}
then for all
\Theta^{1}=\left(\Theta^{1}_{1},\cdots,\Theta_{N}^{1}\right)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\Theta^{2}=\left(\Theta^{2}_{1},\cdots,\Theta_{N}^{2}\right)\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,
\Theta_{k}^{2}\neq 0
on
\supp\Theta_{k}^{1}
,
H^{s_{1}}
-observability for
\Theta^{1}
implies
H^{s_{2}}
-observability for
\Theta^{2}
.

An analogue of Theorem
4
for internal controllability holds, with a simpler proof (see Appendix A). For
N=1
, Theorem
4
follows from the equivalence of
H^{s}
-observability with the celebrated Geometric Control Condition (in short, GCC), as proven in
[
1
]
and
[
3
]
. Here, our proof, that covers any
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, does not rely on the GCC. If
\Theta=\left(\theta,\cdots,\theta\right)
for some
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C})
, then one can expect that
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta
holds if the support of
\theta
fulfils the GCC. In the case of internal controllability, the authors of
[
4
]
characterize
L^{2}
-observability of a system of wave equations, and the condition they obtain is more involved than the usual GCC. Together with the analogue of Theorem
4
for internal controllability, this provides a characterization of internal controllability of some wave systems at every regularity level. Theorem
4
, along with an analogue of
[
4
]
for boundary controllability, would give a characterization of
H^{s}
-exact controllability for
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

An ongoing project with Lauri Oksanen focuses on inverse problems for systems of wave equations. The method employed relies on constructing solutions of (
2
) concentrated within a specific spatial region, both at the regularity level
s=0
and at a high regularity level
s\gg 1
. By Theorem
4
, we only need to check that
L^{2}
-exact controllability holds for the systems considered in that project.

In
[
6
]
, the authors prove a change of regularity result in a very general setup: they find a way to construct smooth controls for smooth data for a time-reversible semi-group. However, the main objective of the article
[
6
]
is to find a control which is defined in the same way at all levels of regularity, and which naturally inherits the regularity of the data. Similarly, in the article
[
5
]
, the authors study the regularity of a fixed control, constructed independently of the regularity of the data. This goal is quite different from ours. Note also that in
[
6
]
, in the case of boundary controllability of a wave equation (
[
6
]
, Theorem 5.4), the regularity of the control at a level of regularity
s\in\mathbb{R}
is

H^{s}((0,T),L^{2}(\partial M))\cap\bigcap_{k=0}^{\lfloor s\rfloor}\mathscr{C}^{k}([0,T],H^{s-k-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial M)),

and not
H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M)
. The result of the present article thus improve upon
[
6
]
with that respect.

Main ideas of the proof.

Consider the simple case
N=1
and
\mathsf{P}=\Delta
. If
u
is a solution of the wave equation with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition, at the Sobolev regularity level
s\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\Delta u
and
\Delta^{-1}u
are solutions of the same wave equation at regularity levels
s-2
and
s+2
. If observability holds for
\Delta u
or
\Delta^{-1}u
, it is natural to wonder whether or not it implies observability for
u
. In the case of
\Delta^{-1}u
, the proof is easy and is essentially based on the ellipticity of
\Delta
. In the other case, the proof relies on the ellipticity of
\partial_{t}^{2}
on the Neumann trace of solutions at the boundary (see Theorem
20
for a precise statement), which can be proved using microlocal techniques.

Outline.

In Section 1, we gather some basic results about the spaces
\mathcal{K}^{s}
and wave systems, and we prove the controllability / observability equivalence (Lemma
3
). In Section 2, we show the ellipticity estimate for
\partial_{t}^{2}
acting on Neumann traces of solutions. In Section 3, we prove Theorem
4
. In Appendix A, we briefly explain how the methods of this article can be adapted to the case of internal observability. Proofs of the results of Section 1 are provided in Appendix B.

Notation.

For
x\in M
and
U,V\in T_{x}M
, we write
\langle U,V\rangle_{g}
for the inner product of
U
and
V
with respect to the metric
g
. The gradient with respect to
g
of a function
u:M\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is denoted by
\nabla u
, and the divergence with respect to
g
of a vector field
X
on
M
is denoted by
\Div X
. We write
\mathrm{d}V_{g}
for the Riemannian density on
M
. Finally,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{X}}
denotes the bilinear duality product between a Banach space
\mathcal{X}
and its dual space
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
denotes the inner product of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, which is linear in the first variable and antilinear in the the second. We write
(\pi_{1},\cdots,\pi_{N})
for the projections associated with the canonical basis of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

Acknowledgements.

I warmly thank Thomas Duyckaerts and Jérôme Le Rousseau for their constant support and guidance. I also thank Lauri Oksanen for introducing me to the questions of exact controllability of systems of wave equations at different levels of regularity, and Sylvain Ervedoza for an enlightening discussion which allowed me to understand correctly the links between
[
6
]
and our results.

Keywords:
systems of wave equations, existence of solutions of hyperbolic equations, regularity of solutions of hyperbolic equations, controllability for systems.

MSC2020:
35L52, 35L53, 35B65, 93B05, 93B07

1
Controllability - observability equivalence

1.1
Adjoint operator

Here, we give the precise expression of the adjoint of
\mathsf{P}
. The operator
X
is compatible with change of coordinates, meaning that for a second set of coordinates
(\tilde{x}^{1},\cdots,\tilde{x}^{n})
, with
X=\tilde{X}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{x}^{i}}
, one has

\tilde{X}^{i}=\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}X^{j},\quad i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket.

(3)

Denote by
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{N})
the canonical basis of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, and take
u=(u^{1},\cdots,u^{N})\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. We use Einstein summation convention in
M
(for indices between
1
and
n
) but not on
\mathbb{C^{N}}
(for indices between
1
and
N
). One writes

Xu=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{N}X_{k\ell}^{j}\frac{\partial u^{\ell}}{\partial x^{j}}e_{k},

where
X_{k\ell}^{j}
is the coefficient
(k,\ell)
of the matrix
X^{j}
. For
k,\ell\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, write
X_{k\ell}=X_{k\ell}^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
, so that

Xu=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{N}\left\langle\nabla u^{\ell},X_{k\ell}\right\rangle_{g}e_{k}.

Using formula (
3
), one sees that
X_{k\ell}
is a vector field for all
k,\ell\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. We will use the notation

\left\langle X,V\right\rangle_{g}u=\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{N}\left\langle X_{k\ell},V\right\rangle_{g}u^{\ell}e_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{N},

if
V
is a vector field on
M
and
u=(u^{1},\cdots,u^{N})
is a function with values in
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. With integration by parts, one derives the following results.

Lemma 5
.

For
u,v\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has

\left\langle Xu,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\left\langle u,X^{\ast}v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\langle\langle X,\nu\rangle_{g}u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

where
X^{\ast}
is the first-order differential operator given by

\left(X^{\ast}v\right)^{\ell}=-\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left\langle\nabla v^{k},\overline{X_{k\ell}}\right\rangle_{g}-\left(\left(\Div\overline{X}\right)v\right)^{\ell},\quad\ell\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket.

Lemma 6
.

For
u,v\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathsf{P}u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\

\displaystyle\left\langle u,\mathsf{P}^{\ast}v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\langle\langle X,\nu\rangle_{g}u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle+\left\langle\partial_{\nu}u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\left\langle u,\partial_{\nu}v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

with
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}=\Delta-X^{\ast}-q^{\ast}
, where
q^{\ast}
denotes the adjoint of
q
.

Remark 7
.

In particular, if
u,v\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, then

\left\langle\mathsf{P}u,v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\left\langle u,\mathsf{P}^{\ast}v\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Remark 8
.

The operators
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
are of the same form. Indeed, set
\tilde{X}=\left(X^{j}\right)^{\ast}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
and let
\tilde{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
be given by
\tilde{q}_{k\ell}=-\Div\overline{X_{\ell k}}+\overline{q_{\ell k}}
for
k,\ell\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. One has
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}=\Delta-\tilde{X}-\tilde{q}
.

1.2
A family of spaces of Sobolev type

Denote by
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}
the action of
\mathsf{P}
on distributions, that is,

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}:\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}),

with
\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}u,\phi\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}}=\langle u,\mathsf{P}^{\ast}\phi\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}}
for
u\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\phi\in\mathscr{D}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

We define a Sobolev-like regularity, adapted to the operator
\mathsf{P}
. Write
\mathcal{K}^{0}=L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and for
m\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, set

\mathcal{K}^{m}=\left\{u\in H^{m}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for }k\in\left\llbracket 0,\left\lfloor\frac{m-1}{2}\right\rfloor\right\rrbracket\right\},

endowed with the
H^{m}
inner product. Here,
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is the floor function. Note that
\mathcal{K}^{1}=H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\mathcal{K}^{2}=H^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, one checks that
\mathcal{K}^{m}
is a complete subspace of
H^{m}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, and, in particular, is a Hilbert space.

The space
\mathcal{K}^{s}
is defined for
s\geq 0
by interpolation. Since the operators
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
are of the same form, one can define the space
\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}
for
s\geq 0
, by replacing
\mathsf{P}
with
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
in the previous definitions. Then, for
s<0
, define
\mathcal{K}^{s}
as the dual of
\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}
as the dual of
\mathcal{K}^{-s}
. Note that for
m\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large,
H_{0}^{m}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is not dense in
\mathcal{K}^{m}
, so that
\mathcal{K}^{-m}\nsubseteq H^{-m}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. For
s\geq 0
, set

\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}=\|u\|_{H^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

and for
s<0
, set

\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}=\sup\left\{\left|\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}\right|,\|v\|_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}\leq 1\right\},

the usual norm of a dual space. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, a norm on
\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}
is defined similarly.

Next, we define the natural action of
\mathsf{P}
on
\mathcal{K}^{s}
. With interpolation, the definition of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
is only needed for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 9

(Definition of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}
)
.

(i)

Suppose
s\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. Then the operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
is the differential operator
\mathsf{P}
on
\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
. It is a bounded operator. The operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{\ast}:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1}
is defined similarly.

(ii)

Suppose
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s\leq-1
. Define
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
as the adjoint of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}^{\ast}:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-1}
, and
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{\ast}:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1}
as the adjoint of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}:\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-s-1}
.

(iii)

If
s=0
, then
\mathcal{K}^{s+1}=\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+1}=H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\mathcal{K}^{s-1}=\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1}=H^{-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. For
u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, define
\mathsf{P}_{\!0}u\in H^{-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

\left\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!0}u,v\right\rangle_{H^{-1},H_{0}^{1}}=-\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left\langle\nabla u^{k},\overline{\nabla v^{k}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}-\left\langle(X+q)u,\overline{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},\quad v\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

This gives an operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!0}:\mathcal{K}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-1}
. The operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!0}^{\ast}:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-1}
is defined similarly.

(iv)

For
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, also define
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{0}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
by

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+r-1}}\mathcal{K}^{s+r-2}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+r-3}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r+3}}\mathcal{K}^{s-r+2}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r+1}}\mathcal{K}^{s-r},

and
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{\ast r}:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-r}
similarly.

Note that for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, as
\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
and
\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
are Hilbert spaces, one has

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}=\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}^{\ast}\right)^{\ast}.

(4)

We check that our definitions make sense in the following lemma.

Lemma 10
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, the operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
is well-defined and continuous. The same is true for
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{\ast r}:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-r}
. If
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
are such that
s-r\geq-1
, then for
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
and
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
such that
v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u
, one has
v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{r}u
in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Proof.

By definition of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}
, we can always assume that
r=1
, and by interpolation, we may assume that
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. The connection between
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}
follows from our definition of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}
for
s\geq 0
.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. For
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
, one has
u\in H^{s+1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}u
in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, implying
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\in H^{s-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and

\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{s+1}}.

Thus, we only have to check that the boundary conditions of the definition of the spaces
\mathcal{K}^{s}
are such that the operators
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}
are well-defined. For
s=0
, the result is true.

Assume that
s
is even and write
s=2\sigma
. By definition,
u\in\mathcal{K}^{2\sigma+1}
gives

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for }k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma\rrbracket.

As
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}u=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u
in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\right)\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for }k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma-1\rrbracket,

that is,
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\in\mathcal{K}^{2\sigma-1}
.

Assume that
s
is odd and write
s=2\sigma+1
. By definition,
u\in\mathcal{K}^{2\sigma+2}
gives

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for }k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma\rrbracket.

If
\sigma=0
, one has
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\in\mathcal{K}^{2\sigma}
. If
\sigma\geq 1
, then as
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}u=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u
in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\right)\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for }k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma-1\rrbracket,

that is,
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\in\mathcal{K}^{2\sigma}
.

Finally, the adjoint of a continuous linear operator between Hilbert spaces is a well-defined continuous operator, so the result is true for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s\leq-1
.
∎

Remark 11
.

(i)

The fact that
v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u
implies
v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{r}u
does not hold for
s<-1
, because
\mathcal{K}^{s}
is not included in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
if
s<-1
.

(ii)

The previous definitions are very natural, but note that some non-intuitive phenomena can occur when dealing with the operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
. To illustrate this, take
N=1
,
\mathsf{P}=\Delta
and
s=-1
. In that case, one has
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{\ast}=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Recall that by definition,
H^{-2}(M)
is the dual of
H_{0}^{2}(M)
. The constant function
u=1
belongs to
L^{2}(M)
, and is sent to zero by the differential operator
\Delta:L^{2}(M)\rightarrow H^{-2}(M)
. However, by definition, the operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-1}:L^{2}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-2}
is the adjoint of the operator

\mathsf{P}_{\!1}:\mathcal{K}^{2}=H^{2}(M)\cap H_{0}^{1}(M)\longrightarrow L^{2}(M),

implying

\left\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-1}u,v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{-2},\mathcal{K}^{2}}=\left\langle 1,\overline{\mathsf{P}_{\!1}v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}=\left\langle 1,\Delta\overline{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}=\left\langle 1,\partial_{\nu}\overline{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\partial M)},\quad v\in\mathcal{K}^{2}.

Hence, the function
u=1
is not sent to zero by the operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-1}:L^{2}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-2}
.

In the following proposition, we gather the properties of the spaces
\mathcal{K}^{s}
that are needed for what follows.

Proposition 12
.

(i)

Embeddings properties.
For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
, the map

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}:\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}

is a well-defined, compact embedding with a dense range. If
s+\delta<0
, the embedding corresponds to a restriction operator. If
s+\delta\geq 0>s
, then the embedding is defined by using
L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
as a pivot space. The operator
\mathsf{P}
commutes with the embeddings: more precisely, for
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
, one has

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+\delta}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}.

(5)

(ii)

Elliptic estimate of
\mathsf{P}
.
Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, and
w\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}
. We already know that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}w\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}
. Assume that there exists
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
such that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}w=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}(v)
. Then there exists
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
such that

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}(u)=w\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u=v.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
such that

\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}\leq C\left(\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}\right),\quad u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}.

(6)

(iii)

The shift operator.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, there exists a continuous isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
such that the following property holds: for
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
,

\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r+r^{\prime}}=\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r^{\prime}}^{r}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r}^{r^{\prime}}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r+r^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-r^{\prime}},

(7)

\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1}^{r}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+r}=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1},

and

\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\delta}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}.

(8)

In addition, for
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, one has

\left\|\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r}\right)u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}\leq C_{r,s}\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}},\quad u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r},

(9)

for some
C_{r,s}>0
. The operator
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}
will be defined by
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}
, for
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
chosen sufficiently large.

Remark 13
.

If we omit the embedding notation, then
(ii)
can be written as

u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}\text{ and }\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}.

Note that we cannot replace
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
by
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{r}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
. With the same example as in Remark
11
, take
N=1
,
\mathsf{P}=\Delta
,
s=0
,
r=1
, and let
u
be the constant function
u=1\in\mathcal{K}^{0}=L^{2}(M)
. One has
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}u=0
, implying
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}u\in\mathcal{K}^{-1}=H^{-1}(M)
. However,
u
does not belong to the space
\mathcal{K}^{1}=H_{0}^{1}(M)
.

Remark 14
.

By definition, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
, one has

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}=\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-\delta}}\right)^{\ast}.

(10)

The proof of Proposition
12
is given in appendix. The proof of our main result will use the following interpolation lemma.

Lemma 15
.

For
\eta\in[0,1]
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, one has
\left[\mathcal{K}^{s+2},\mathcal{K}^{s}\right]_{\eta}=\mathcal{K}^{s+2-2\eta}
, with equivalent norms, where
\left[\mathcal{K}^{s+2},\mathcal{K}^{s}\right]_{\eta}
denotes the complex interpolation space between
\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
and
\mathcal{K}^{s}
.

Proof.

First, note that the result is standard for
s\in[-2,0]
, as
\mathcal{K}^{s}=D(\Delta_{\mathrm{dir}}^{\frac{s}{2}})^{N}
for
s\in[-2,2]
. Second, we prove Lemma
15
for
s>0
, using the shift operator and the definition of complex interpolation spaces (see, for example,
[
2
]
). If
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
are subspaces of a Banach space
\mathcal{X}
, we write
\mathscr{F}_{A_{0},A_{1}}
for the set of continuous functions
f:\left\{z\in\mathbb{C},0\leq\operatorname{Re}z\leq 1\right\}\rightarrow A_{0}+A_{1}
satisfying the following two properties:
f
is analytic on the open strip
\left\{z\in\mathbb{C},0<\operatorname{Re}z<1\right\}
, and for
j=0,1
, the function
t\mapsto f(j+it)
maps continuously
\mathbb{R}
to
A_{j}
, and tends to zero as
|t|
tends to infinity. To ease notation, we omit embeddings and subscripts of the shift operator, identifying
\mathcal{K}^{s}
and
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-2}}\left(\mathcal{K}^{s}\right)
, for
s\geq-2
, and writing
\mathcal{S}^{k}=\mathcal{S}_{k-2}^{k}:\mathcal{K}^{2k-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-2}
. Consider
s>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s-2k\in[-2,0]
, and
\eta\in[0,1]
. By definition, one has

\left[\mathcal{K}^{s+2},\mathcal{K}^{s}\right]_{\eta}=\left\{u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}+\mathcal{K}^{s},u=f(\eta)\text{ for some }f\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2},\mathcal{K}^{s}}\right\}.

As
\left\{\mathcal{S}^{k}\circ f,f\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2},\mathcal{K}^{s}}\right\}=\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2-2k},\mathcal{K}^{s-2k}}
, one has

\displaystyle u\in\left[\mathcal{K}^{s+2},\mathcal{K}^{s}\right]_{\eta}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{k}u=v\text{ for some }v\in\left[\mathcal{K}^{s+2-2k},\mathcal{K}^{s-2k}\right]_{\eta},

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow u=\left(\mathcal{S}^{k}\right)^{-1}v\text{ for some }v\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2-2k-2\eta},

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2-2\eta},

by the case
s\in[-2,0]
, and Proposition
12
. Third, for
s<-2
, using Corollary 4.5.2 and Theorem 4.2.1 of
[
2
]
, one obtains
\left(\left[\mathcal{K}^{s+2},\mathcal{K}^{s}\right]_{\eta}\right)^{\prime}=\left[\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-2}\right]_{1-\eta}=\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-2+2\eta}
, as
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
are of the same form. This completes the proof.
∎

1.3
Solutions of the wave equations

Most of the ideas used here can be found in
[
8
]
. For wave equations with Dirichlet boundary condition, one has the following theorem.

Theorem 16
.

Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
. There exists a unique

u\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-1})

such that
\left(u(0),\partial_{t}u(0)\right)=\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
and
\partial_{t}^{2}u(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We will say that
u
is the solution of the wave equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&0&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }\mathbb{R}\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

The following additional results hold.

(i)

One has

u\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1-k}),

and
\partial_{t}^{2k}u(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1-k}^{k}u(t)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1-2k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
T>0
, there exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\partial_{t}^{k}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s+1-k})}\leq C\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}},\quad\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}.

In particular, if
s\geq-2
, then
u\in H^{s+1}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for all
T>0
, with the corresponding inequality.

(ii)

For
\delta>0
, if
\tilde{u}
is the solution with initial data

\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1-\delta}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-\delta}}u^{1}\right),

then for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, one has
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1-\delta}}u(t)=\tilde{u}(t)
. In particular, a solution can be approximated by solutions with higher regularity.

(iii)

Consider
T>0
. A normal derivative
\partial_{\nu}u
at the boundary, that lies in
H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, can be defined extending the usual normal derivative if
u
is sufficiently smooth. For
\delta>0
, one has

\partial_{\nu}\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1-\delta}}u\right)=\iota_{H^{s}\rightarrow H^{s-\delta}}\partial_{\nu}u,

where
\iota_{H^{s}\rightarrow H^{s-\delta}}
denotes the embedding from
H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
into
H^{s-\delta}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. There exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}},\quad\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\partial_{t}^{2k}u
is the solution associated with
\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1-k}^{k}u^{0},\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-k}^{k}u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1-2k}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-2k}
, and one has

\partial_{\nu}\partial_{t}^{2k}u=\partial_{t}^{2k}\partial_{\nu}u\in H^{s-2k}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(iv)

Assume that
s\geq 0
. For
F\in L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, we define the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&F&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&0&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

using the Duhamel formula. One has
u\in\mathscr{C}^{0}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s})
,
\partial_{\nu}u\in H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, and there exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\left(u,\partial_{t}u\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}((0,T),H^{s})},

for all
F\in L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. If in addition
F\in\mathscr{C}^{0}((0,T),H^{s-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, then
u\in\mathscr{C}^{2}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s-1})
, with
\partial_{t}^{2}u=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u+F
and

\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s-1})}\leq C\left(\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}+\left\|F\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),H^{s-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\right),

for some
C>0
independent of
F
.

Using Theorem
16
-
(i)
, Theorem
16
-
(ii)
and Proposition
12
-
(iii)
, one obtains the following corollary.

Corollary 17
.

Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
, and denote by
u
the solution with initial data
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
, given by Theorem
16
. Then,
w=\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}u
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}w-\mathsf{P}w&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(w(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}w(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(w^{0},w^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
w&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

where
\left(w^{0},w^{1}\right)=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}u^{0},\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r}^{r}u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}
.

For wave equations with inhomogeneous boundary condition, one has the following theorem.

Theorem 18
.

Consider
T>0
,
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
f\in H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. If
s\leq 0
, we define the solution of the wave equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}v-\mathsf{P}^{\ast}v&=&0&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times M,\\
\left(v(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}v(0,\cdot)\right)&=&0&&\text{in }M,\\
v&=&\diag(\Theta)f&&\text{on }\mathbb{R}\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

(11)

by duality with Theorem
16
-(iv):
v
is the unique element of
L^{\infty}((0,T),H^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
such that

\left\langle v,F\right\rangle_{L^{\infty}(H^{s}),L^{1}(H_{0}^{-s})}=-\left\langle f,\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\rangle_{H^{s},H_{0}^{-s}},

for all
F\in L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{-s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, where
u
is the solution associated with
F
defined in Theorem
16
-(iv). If
s>0
, we define the solution of the previous wave equation as in the case
s=0
. In any case, one has

v\in\mathscr{C}^{0}((0,T),H^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}((0,T),H^{s-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}((0,T),H^{s-2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})),

\partial_{t}^{2}v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{\ast}v
in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, and there exists
C>0
such that

\sum_{j=0}^{2}\left\|\partial_{t}^{j}v\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],H^{s-j})}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},\quad f\in H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

If
s\geq 1
, then
v(t)_{|\partial M}=\left(\diag(\Theta)f\right)_{|\{t\}\times\partial M}
in
H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, in the sense of classical Sobolev trace operators. In addition, as
\Theta
is compactly supported in
(0,T)\times\partial M
, one has
\left(v(T),\partial_{t}v(T)\right)\in\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1}
, with the following duality equality: for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s}
, if
u
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(T,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(T,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

then

\left\langle u^{1},v(T)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{-s+1},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1}}-\left\langle u^{0},\partial_{t}v(T)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{-s},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\left\langle\partial_{\nu}u,\diag(\Theta)f\right\rangle_{H^{-s},H_{0}^{s}}&\text{ if }s\geq 0\\
\left\langle f,\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\rangle_{H^{s},H_{0}^{-s}}&\text{ if }s<0\end{array}\right..

(12)

The proof of Theorems
16
and
18
is given in appendix.

1.4
The duality argument

Here, we prove Lemma
3
. The proof is based on the following classical result (see for example Corollary 11.20 of
[
9
]
).

Theorem 19
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be Hilbert spaces, and
K:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a linear continuous operator. Then
K
is surjective if and only if there exists
C>0
such that

\|y\|_{\mathcal{Y}}\leq C\|K^{\ast}y\|_{\mathcal{X}},\quad y\in\mathcal{Y}.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, if one denotes by
H_{0}^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
the closure of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
in
H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, then
H_{0}^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
is the dual of
H_{0}^{-s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
T>0
and
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. By Theorem
18
, one can define

\begin{array}[]{cccc}K:&H_{0}^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{K}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-1}\\
&f&\longmapsto&(v(T),\partial_{t}v(T))\end{array}

where
v
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}v-\mathsf{P}^{\ast}v&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(v(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}v(0,\cdot)\right)&=&0&&\text{in }M,\\
v&=&\diag(\Theta)f&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

Note that in the definition of
H^{s}
-exact controllability, one can consider
f\in H_{0}^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
instead of
H^{s}(0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Hence,
H^{s}
-exact controllability for
\Theta
holds if and only if the operator
K
is surjective. By Theorem
18
, the adjoint of
K
is given by

\begin{array}[]{cccc}K^{\ast}:&\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s}&\longrightarrow&H_{0}^{-s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\\
&(u^{1},u^{0})&\longmapsto&\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\end{array}

where
u
is the solution

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(T,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(T,\cdot)\right)&=&(-u^{0},u^{1})&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

(13)

By Theorem
19
,
H^{s}
-exact controllability for
\Theta
is equivalent with the inequality

\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{-s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},\quad\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s},

(14)

where
u
is the solution of (
13
). One has

\left\|\left(u(0),\partial_{t}u(0)\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s}}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s}}\lesssim\left\|\left(u(0),\partial_{t}u(0)\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s}},

for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{-s}
, where
u
is the solution of (
13
), implying that (
14
) and
H^{-s}
-observability are equivalent. This completes the proof of Lemma
3
.

2
Ellipticity of the time-derivative on Neumann traces

2.1
Statement of the main estimate and beginning of the proof

The main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 20

(Ellipticity of the time-derivative on the Neumann trace)
.

For
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
s>-1
, and
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, there exists
C>0
such that for all
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
, one has

\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left(\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\right)

where
u
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&0&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }\mathbb{R}\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

Remark 21
.

For clarity, embeddings have been omitted in the statement of Theorem
20
. The notation
\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}
stands for

\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}.

Proof.

Let
(O^{j})_{j\in J}
be a finite family of open subsets of
M
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

One has

\bigcup_{j\in J}\left(O^{j}\cap\partial M\right)=\partial M.

(ii)

For each
j\in J
, there exists a smooth diffeomorphism
\kappa^{j}
such that

\kappa^{j}:\tilde{O}^{j}\longrightarrow O^{j}\cap\partial M

where
\tilde{O}^{j}
is a non-empty subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

(iii)

We can use boundary normal coordinates on each
O^{j}
: more precisely, we assume that there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
j\in J
, the map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\tilde{\kappa}^{j}:&\tilde{O}^{j}\times[0,\delta)&\longrightarrow&O^{j}\\
&(x^{\prime},x^{n})&\longmapsto&\gamma(\nu_{\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime})},x^{n})\end{array}

is a smooth diffeomorphism, where for
y\in\partial M
,
\nu_{y}
is the inward-pointing unit vector normal to the boundary at
y
, and
\gamma(\nu_{\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime})},\cdot)
is the geodesic starting from
\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime})
and of initial velocity
\nu_{\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime})}
.

It is well-know that in the coordinates given by
\tilde{\kappa}^{j}
, the Laplace-Beltrami operator becomes an elliptic operator
\tilde{P}^{j}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
with principal part

\tilde{P}^{j}=\partial_{x^{n}}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq p,q\leq n-1}\alpha_{j}^{pq}(x)\partial_{x^{p}}\partial_{x^{q}}.

(15)

The coefficients
(\alpha_{j}^{pq})
can be smoothly extended to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in such a way that
\tilde{P}^{j}
is an elliptic operator on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We take a partition of the unity associated to the sets
(O^{j})_{j\in J}
: there exists a family of functions
(\psi^{j})_{j\in J}
such that for each
j\in J
,
\psi^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(O^{j},[0,1])
and such that

\sum_{j\in J}(\psi^{j})^{2}=1

in a neighborhood of
\partial M
in
M
. Also, take
\psi^{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T),[0,1])
such that
\psi^{0}\Theta=\Theta
.

Consider
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
. We start the proof by writing

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle=\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle=\left\|\psi^{0}\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in J}\left\|(\psi^{j})^{2}\psi^{0}\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times(O^{j}\cap\partial M),\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

(16)

For
j\in J
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define

u^{j}(t,x)=\psi^{0}(t)\psi^{j}(\tilde{\kappa}^{j}(x))u(t,\tilde{\kappa}^{j}(x))\quad\text{ and }\quad\Theta^{j}(t,x^{\prime})=\psi^{j}(\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime}))\Theta(t,\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime})).

Note that those functions are well-defined because
\psi^{j}
is compactly supported in
O^{j}
. As
u_{|\partial M}=0
, one has

\partial_{\nu}u^{j}(t,x^{\prime},0)=\psi^{0}(t)\psi^{j}\left(\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime})\right)\partial_{\nu}u\left(t,\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime})\right).

(17)

By definition of the
H^{s}-
norm on a Riemannian manifold, coming back to (
16
), we thus have

\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\sum_{j\in J}\left\|\diag(\Theta^{j})\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\tilde{O}^{j},\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\sum_{j\in J}\left\|\diag(\Theta^{j})\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Recall that
(\pi_{1},\cdots,\pi_{N})
denotes the projections associated with the canonical basis of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. By definition of the
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})
-norm, one has

\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{j\in J}\left\|\left(\pi_{k}\Theta^{j}\right)\partial_{\nu}\left(\pi_{k}u^{j}\right)\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

(18)

We see that we are reduced to the study of scalar functions defined on the half-space
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. We gather the properties satisfied by the functions
\pi_{k}u^{j}
. First, as
s\geq-2
, one has
\pi_{k}u^{j}\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
by Theorem
16
. Second, one has
\pi_{k}u^{j}(t,x^{\prime},0)=0
for all
(t,x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
by the Dirichlet boundary condition, and

\partial_{\nu}\left(\pi_{k}u^{j}\right)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})

by Theorem
16
and (
17
). Third, we know that

\partial_{t}^{2}u-\Delta u=-Xu-qu

where
\Delta
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator, so by the Leibniz formula, there exists a differential operator
R^{j}
of order
1
, supported in
(0,T)\times O^{j}
such that

\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\tilde{P}^{j}\right)u^{j}(t,x)=R^{j}u(t,\tilde{\kappa}^{j}(x))

where
\tilde{P}^{j}
is defined by (
15
). In particular, one has

\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\tilde{P}^{j}\right)(\pi_{k}u^{j})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}).

Proposition 22
.

Suppose

P=\partial_{t}^{2}-\partial_{x^{n}}^{2}-\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n-1}\alpha^{ij}(x)\partial_{x^{i}}\partial_{x^{j}}

on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
, where the coefficients
(\alpha^{ij})
are such that

\xi_{n}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n-1}\alpha^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}

is uniformly elliptic on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Take
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C})
. There exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\theta\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\leq\

\displaystyle C\left(\left\|\theta\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|Pu\right\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\|u\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\right).

for all
u\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
such that
Pu\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
,
u_{|x_{n}=0}=0
and
\partial_{\nu}u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
.

A proof is given in section 3.2. This proposition allows us to complete the proof of Theorem
20
. One obtains

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\pi_{k}\Theta^{j}\right)\partial_{\nu}\pi_{k}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\pi_{k}\Theta^{j}\right)\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}\pi_{k}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\tilde{P}^{j}\right)(\pi_{k}u^{j})\right\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{\nu}\pi_{k}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|\pi_{k}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}.

Using (
18
) and the definition of the
H^{s}
-norms of vectors, one has

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\sum_{j\in J}\left\|\diag(\Theta^{j})\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\|\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\tilde{P}^{j}\right)u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right).

We estimate the terms on the right-hand side one by one.

First term.

We prove

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J}\left\|\diag(\Theta^{j})\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

(19)

meaning that the first term yields the main term of the estimate up to a remainder term.

Using (
17
) and the Leibniz formula, one finds

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta^{j})\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{2r}\left\|\diag(\Theta^{j})\left(\psi^{j}\circ\kappa^{j}\right)\partial_{t}^{k}\psi^{0}\partial_{t}^{2r-k}\partial_{\nu}u(t,\kappa^{j}(x^{\prime}))\right\|_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

As
\psi^{j}
is supported in a coordinate chart of
\partial M
, one has

\left\|\diag(\Theta^{j})\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{2r}\left\|\diag(\Theta)\left(\psi^{j}\right)^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi^{0}\partial_{t}^{2r-k}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

One has

\left\|\diag(\Theta)\left(\psi^{j}\right)^{2}\psi^{0}\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

and for
k\in\llbracket 1,2r\rrbracket
,

\left\|\diag(\Theta)\left(\psi^{j}\right)^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi^{0}\partial_{t}^{2r-k}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

This gives (
19
).

Second term.

For the second term, one has

\sum_{j\in J}\left\|\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\tilde{P}^{j}\right)u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

This holds since for all
j
, there exists a differential operator
R^{j}
of order
1
, supported in
(0,T)\times O^{j}
such that

\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\tilde{P}^{j}\right)u^{j}(t,x)=R^{j}u(t,\tilde{\kappa}^{j}(x)).

Third and forth term.

Arguing as above, one finds

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J}\left(\left\|\partial_{\nu}u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|u^{j}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Conclusion.

Gathering all our estimates, one finds

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

By Theorem
16
, one has

\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\|u^{0}\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}+\|u^{1}\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}

and

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right)+1}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\|u^{0}\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\|u^{1}\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}},

as
s-\frac{1}{2}\geq-2
. This completes the proof.
∎

2.2
Analysis in a half-space.

Here, we prove Proposition
22
. Write
\mathsf{S}^{m}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
for the set of symbols of order
m
,
\mathsf{S}_{\intercal}^{m}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
for the set of tangential symbols of order
m
, and
\Psi^{m}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
and
\Psi^{m}_{\intercal}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
for the associated sets of pseudo-differential operators. Let
p
be the principal symbol of
P
, that is

p(x,\tau,\xi)=-\tau^{2}+\xi_{n}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n-1}\alpha^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}.

Write

|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n-1}\alpha^{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\quad\text{ and }\quad\rho(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})=-\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}

so that
p(x,\tau,\xi)=\xi_{n}^{2}+\rho(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})
. On the boundary, we sometimes use the notation
|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}=|\xi^{\prime}|_{(x^{\prime},0)}
.

Consider
u\in H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
satisfying the assumptions of Proposition
22
. The idea of the proof is to split miscellaneously
\theta\partial_{\nu}u
into two terms: one on which
\partial_{t}
is elliptic, and one on which the wave operator is elliptic. More precisely, we fix
\chi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},[0,1])
such that
\chi_{0}=1
on
\left[\frac{1}{4},+\infty\right)
and
\chi_{0}=0
on
\left[0,\frac{1}{5}\right]
, and we define

\chi(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})=\chi_{0}\left(\frac{\rho(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})}{1+\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}}\right).

Then
\chi\in\mathsf{S}_{\intercal}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
by Lemma 18.1.10 of
[
7
]
. One has
\chi(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})=1
if
1+\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}\leq 4\rho(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})
, and
\chi
is supported in the set

\left\{(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},1+\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}\leq 5\rho(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\right\}.

One can write

\left\|\theta\partial_{\nu}u\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\left\|\theta\OpT(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

We will study the two terms on the right-hand side separately: for the first one,
\partial_{t}
turns out to be elliptic, and for the second one,
P
is elliptic.

Remark 23
.

We use the notation
\OpT
both for tangential pseudo-differential operators on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and for pseudo-differential operators on the boundary
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}}
. They coincide at
x_{n}=0
up to a
\frac{1}{2\pi}
factor.

2.2.1
Ellipticity of the time-derivative

We prove the following estimate.

Lemma 24
.

There exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\theta\OpT(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\leq C\left(\left\|\theta\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|^{2}_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|^{2}_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\right).

Proof.

Fix
\chi_{1}=\chi_{1}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})
a smooth compactly supported function such that
\chi_{1}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})=1
if
\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}\leq 1
. Write

\displaystyle\left\|\theta\OpT(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\leq

\displaystyle\ \left\|\theta\OpT\left(\chi_{1}(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\right)(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

\displaystyle+\left\|\theta\OpT\left((1-\chi_{1})(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\right)(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

As
\chi_{1}(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\in\mathsf{S}^{-\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
, one has

\left\|\theta\OpT\left(\chi_{1}(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\right)(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|^{2}_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

Thus, to complete the proof of the lemma, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\left\|\theta\OpT\left((1-\chi_{1})(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\right)(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\

\displaystyle\left\|\theta\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|^{2}_{H^{s-2r}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|^{2}_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

(20)

Set
\chi_{2}=(1-\chi_{1})(1-\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\in\mathsf{S}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
. If
(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})\in\supp\chi_{2}
, then
\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}>1
and

1+\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}>4\left(|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}-\tau^{2}\right).

Combining those two inequalities, one finds

2\left(\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}\right)+1+\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}>2+4\left(|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}-\tau^{2}\right)

that is

7\tau^{2}>1+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}.

(21)

In particular,
\tilde{\chi}_{2}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})=\tau^{-2r}\chi_{2}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})
is well-defined. Using (
21
), one finds
\tilde{\chi}_{2}\in\mathsf{S}^{-2r}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
. Since
\OpT(\chi_{2})=\OpT(\tilde{\chi}_{2})\partial_{t}^{2r}
, one has

\displaystyle\left\|\theta\OpT(\chi_{2})(\partial_{\nu}u)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\leq\

\displaystyle\left\|\OpT(\tilde{\chi}_{2})\left(\theta\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

\displaystyle+\left\|\left[\theta,\OpT(\tilde{\chi}_{2})\right]\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

and this gives (
20
), as
\left[\theta,\OpT(\tilde{\chi}_{2})\right]\partial_{t}^{2r}\in\Psi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
.
∎

2.2.2
Ellipticity of the wave operator

We denote by
\underline{u}
and
\underline{f}
the extensions by
0
of
u
and
f
on the whole space. In the sense of distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, since
u_{|x_{n}=0}=0
, one has

P\underline{u}=\underline{f}+\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\partial_{\nu}u,

and this holds in fact in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. As
\OpT(\chi)
sends
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
to
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, one has

P\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}+\left[\OpT(\chi),P\right]\underline{u}=\OpT(\chi)\underline{f}+\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\left(\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right)

(22)

in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
.

To get an estimate on
\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u
, we apply a parametrix of
P
. Thus, one has to find a non-tangential symbol
\tilde{\chi}
of order
0
supported where
P
is elliptic, and such that
\tilde{\chi}(x,\tau,\xi)=1
if
(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\in\supp\chi
. If
(x,\tau,\xi)
is such that
(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\in\supp\chi
, then

1+\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}\leq 5\left(|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}-\tau^{2}\right)

and this implies

1+\tau^{2}+\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}\leq 5\left(|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}+\xi_{n}^{2}-\tau^{2}\right)=5p(x,\tau,\xi).

Set

\tilde{\chi}(x,\tau,\xi)=\eta\left(\frac{p(x,\tau,\xi)}{1+\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}+\tau^{2}}\right)

where
\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},[0,1])
is such that
\eta(\sigma)=1
if
\sigma\geq\frac{1}{5}
, and
\eta(\sigma)=0
if
\sigma\leq\frac{1}{10}
. Then
\tilde{\chi}
is supported where
P
is elliptic, and
\tilde{\chi}(x,\tau,\xi)=1
if
(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\in\supp\chi
. The function
\tilde{\chi}
is a symbol of order 0 by Lemma 18.1.10 of
[
7
]
.

Set
Q=\Op(q)\in\Psi^{-2}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
, with

q(x,\tau,\xi)=\frac{\tilde{\chi}(x,\tau,\xi)}{p(x,\tau,\xi)}.

Pseudo-differential calculus gives
QP=\Op(\tilde{\chi})+R_{1}
, with
R_{1}\in\Psi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
. Note that one can construct
\tilde{Q}\in\Psi^{-2}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
such that

\tilde{Q}P-\Op(\tilde{\chi})\in\Psi^{-\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})

as in Theorem 18.1.9 of
[
7
]
, but such a refinement is not needed here.

Applying
Q
to Equation (
22
), one finds

\Op(\tilde{\chi})\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}+R_{1}\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}+Q\left[\OpT(\chi),P\right]\underline{u}=Q\OpT(\chi)\underline{f}+Q\left(\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\left(\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right)\right).

(23)

Since
u_{|x_{n}=0}=0
, one has

\Op(\tilde{\chi})\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}_{|x_{n}=0}=\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}_{|x_{n}=0}+\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}_{|x_{n}=0}=\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}_{|x_{n}=0}.

Thus, computing the trace of (
23
) at
x^{n}=0
gives

Q\left(\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\left(\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right)\right)_{|x_{n}=0}=-Q\OpT(\chi)\underline{f}_{|x_{n}=0}+R_{3}u_{|x_{n}=0}

(24)

where the rest
R_{3}u
is

R_{3}u=\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}+R_{1}\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}+Q\left[\OpT(\chi),P\right]\underline{u}.

Lemma 25
.

There exists
C>0
such that

\left\|Q\OpT(\chi)\underline{f}_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\right)

(25)

and

\left\|R_{3}u_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\leq C\left(\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\|u\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\right).

(26)

Proof.

As
s>-1
and as
Q
is of order
-2
, one has

\left\|Q\OpT(\chi)\underline{f}_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\left\|Q\OpT(\chi)\underline{f}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\lesssim\left\|\OpT(\chi)\underline{f}\right\|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}.

As
\chi\in\Psi_{\intercal}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
, one obtains (
25
). Next, we prove (
26
).

Term 1.

For the term
\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}
, we use Theorem 18.1.35 of
[
7
]
.

Lemma 26
.

The symbol
1-\tilde{\chi}
satisfies the assumption of Theorem 18.1.35 of
[
7
]
: there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

1-\tilde{\chi}(x,\tau,\xi)=0

if
\varepsilon|\xi_{n}|>1
and
|(\tau,\xi^{\prime})|\leq\varepsilon|\xi_{n}|
.

Proof.

There exists
C>0
such that

|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}\leq c|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}

for all
(x,\xi^{\prime})
. Hence, if
\varepsilon|\xi_{n}|>1
and
|(\tau,\xi^{\prime})|\leq\varepsilon|\xi_{n}|
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}-\tau^{2}}{1+\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}+\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\xi_{n}^{2}-\tau^{2}}{1+\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}+\tau^{2}}\geq\frac{\xi_{n}^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}}{1+\xi_{n}^{2}+c\varepsilon^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}}\geq\frac{\xi_{n}^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}+\xi_{n}^{2}+c\varepsilon^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\xi_{n}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1-\varepsilon^{2}}{1+c\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon^{2}}.

Thus, if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small, one has

\frac{\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}-\tau^{2}}{1+\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}+\tau^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{5}

implying
\tilde{\chi}(x,\tau,\xi)=1
.
∎

Thus, one has
\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\in\Psi^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
, with vanishing symbol. Hence,
\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\in\Psi^{-\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
, yielding

\left\|\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\left\|\Op(\tilde{\chi}-1)\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{3}{2}-N}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}

for any
N\geq 0
.

Term 2.

For the term
R_{1}\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}
, as
R_{1}\in\Psi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
, one has

\left\|R_{1}\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\left\|R_{1}\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}.

Term 3.

Pseudo-differential calculus gives
Q\left[\OpT(\chi),P\right]\in\Psi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x})
, yielding

\displaystyle\left\|Q\left[\OpT(\chi),P\right]\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|Q\left[\OpT(\chi),P\right]\OpT(\chi)\underline{u}\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}.

Gathering all those estimates, one finds (
26
). This completes the proof of Lemma
25
.
∎

We now turn to the study of the left-hand side of (
24
).

Lemma 27
.

There exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle C\left(\left\|Q\left(\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\left(\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right)\right)_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\right).

Proof.

The idea is to find a pseudo-differential expression of

\Op(q)\left(\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\left(\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right)\right)_{|x^{n}=0}.

By definition, one has

\Op(q)\left(\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\left(\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right)\right)_{|x_{n}=0}(t,x^{\prime})=\OpT(q_{\intercal})\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u(t,x)

(27)

where
q_{\intercal}
is the symbol

q_{\intercal}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\tilde{\chi}(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi)}{\xi_{n}^{2}+\rho(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}\xi_{n}.

Lemma 28
.

One has
q_{\intercal}\in\mathsf{S}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

Note that an explicit formula for
q_{\intercal}
is not needed. The idea of the proof is to write
q_{\intercal}=a\times\left(F\circ b\right)
where
a
is a symbol of order
-1
,
F
is a smooth function, and
b
is a symbol of order
0
, so that the conclusion will be a consequence of Lemma 18.1.10 of
[
7
]
. Recall that

\tilde{\chi}(x,\tau,\xi)=\eta\left(\frac{p(x,\tau,\xi)}{1+\xi_{n}^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}+\tau^{2}}\right)

where
\eta
is a real nonnegative smooth function such that
\eta(\sigma)=1
if
\sigma\geq\frac{1}{5}
, and
\eta(\sigma)=0
if
\sigma\leq\frac{1}{10}
. Write
|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}
instead of
|\xi^{\prime}|_{(x^{\prime},0)}
, and set

b(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})=\frac{|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}-\tau^{2}}{1+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}+\tau^{2}}.

Then
b\in\mathsf{S}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
and a change of variable gives

q_{\intercal}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}+\tau^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}+b(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})}\eta\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}+b(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})}{\sigma^{2}+1}\right)\mathrm{d}\sigma.

Set

F(\sigma^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{\prime}}\eta\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{\prime}}{\sigma^{2}+1}\right)\mathrm{d}\sigma.

Lemma 29
.

The function
F
is smooth.

Proof.

Consider
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and write

g(\sigma)=\frac{\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{\prime}}{\sigma^{2}+1}

for
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that if
\sigma^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{5}
, then
g(\sigma)\geq\frac{1}{5}
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, so that

F(\sigma^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}\sigma.

Hence, we may assume that
\sigma^{\prime}<\frac{1}{5}
. In particular, as
\sigma^{\prime}<1
, the function
g
is a bijection from
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
[\sigma^{\prime},1]
. A change of variables gives

F(\sigma^{\prime})=\int_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{1}\frac{\eta(\sigma)}{\sigma\sqrt{1-\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\sigma.

As
\eta(\sigma)=0
for
\sigma\leq\frac{1}{10}
, one finds that if
\sigma^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{10}
, then

F(\sigma^{\prime})=\int_{\frac{1}{10}}^{1}\frac{\eta(\sigma)}{\sigma\sqrt{1-\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}}}\mathrm{d}\sigma,

implying that
F
is smooth on
\left(-\infty,\frac{1}{10}\right]
. Finally, note that

F(\sigma^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\eta\left(\sigma^{\prime}+(1-\sigma^{\prime})\sigma\right)}{(\sigma^{\prime}+(1-\sigma^{\prime})\sigma)\sqrt{1-\sigma}\sqrt{\sigma}}\mathrm{d}\sigma,

by a last change of variable. As
\sigma^{\prime}+(1-\sigma^{\prime})\sigma>\frac{1}{10}
for
\sigma\in(0,1)
, this proves that
F
is smooth on
[\frac{1}{10},\frac{1}{5}]
.
∎

Lemma 18.1.10 of
[
7
]
gives
q_{\intercal}\in\mathsf{S}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
, completing the proof of Lemma
28
.
∎

With the same construction as for
\chi
, consider
\chi_{3}\in\mathsf{S}^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
such that
\chi_{3}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})=1
if
(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\in\supp\chi
and
1+\tau^{2}+|\xi^{\prime}|_{x}^{2}\lesssim\rho(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})
on
\supp\chi_{3}
. The function
\tilde{\chi}_{3}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi^{\prime})=\sqrt{\rho(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})}\chi_{3}(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})
is well-defined, and one has
\tilde{\chi}_{3}\in\mathsf{S}^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{x^{\prime}})
. By Lemma
28
, one obtains

\OpT\left(\tilde{\chi}_{3}\right)\OpT(q_{\intercal})\OpT(\chi_{|x_{n}=0})=\OpT\left(\sqrt{\rho}q_{\intercal}\chi_{|x_{n}=0}\right)+R_{4}

where
R_{4}
is a tangential pseudo-differential operator of order
-1
. As
\tilde{\chi}(x^{\prime},\tau,\xi)=1
if
(x,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\in\supp\chi
, one has

q_{\intercal}(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\chi(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})=\chi(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\xi_{n}^{2}+\rho(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}\xi_{n}=\frac{\pi\chi(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})}{\sqrt{\rho(x^{\prime},0,\tau,\xi^{\prime})}},

and this gives

\OpT\left(\tilde{\chi}_{3}\right)\OpT(q_{\intercal})\OpT(\chi_{|x_{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u=\pi\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u+R_{4}\partial_{\nu}u.

This yields

\left\|\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\left\|\OpT\left(\tilde{\chi}_{3}\right)\OpT(q_{\intercal})\OpT(\chi_{|x_{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|R_{4}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

As
\tilde{\chi}_{3}
is of order
1
and
R_{4}
of order
-1
, this gives

\left\|\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\left\|\OpT(q_{\intercal})\OpT(\chi_{|x_{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

Using (
27
), one obtains

\displaystyle\left\|\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\Op(q)\left(\delta_{x^{n}=0}\otimes\left(\OpT(\chi_{|x^{n}=0})\partial_{\nu}u\right)\right)_{|x^{n}=0}\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
27
.
∎

Using Lemma
25
and Lemma
27
in (
24
), one finds the estimate of Proposition
22
.

3
Change of regularity in observability inequalities

We prove our main result, Theorem
4
, that is, the equivalence between observability at different levels of regularity. First, we show that for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta
implies
H^{s+2r}
-observability for
\Theta
. Second, we show that for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
s>-1
,
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta
implies
H^{s-2r}
-observability for
\tilde{\Theta}
, for all
\tilde{\Theta}
such that
\pi_{k}\tilde{\Theta}\neq 0
on
\supp\pi_{k}\Theta
, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. This is enough to give the full conclusion, by Lemma
15
.

3.1
Increasing the level of regularity

Consider
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and assume that
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta
holds. We prove that
H^{s+2}
-observability for
\Theta
holds, implying by induction that
H^{s+2r}
-observability for
\Theta
holds for all
r\geq 1
. Consider also
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
, and write
u
for the solution with initial data
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
. We show that

\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s+2}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

For
t\in(0,T)
, set
\tilde{u}(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+2}u(t)
. Then,
\tilde{u}
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{u}-\mathsf{P}\tilde{u}&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(\tilde{u}(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+2}u^{0},\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
\tilde{u}&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

Since
\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+2}u^{0},\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
,
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta
gives

\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+2}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}+\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

By Theorem
16
, one has
\tilde{u}(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+2}u(t)=\partial_{t}^{2}u(t)
in
\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and
\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}=\partial_{\nu}\partial_{t}^{2}u=\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{\nu}u
. Hence, the previous estimate reads

\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+2}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}+\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Using the ellipticity estimate for
\mathsf{P}
(Proposition
12
-
(ii)
), one finds

\displaystyle\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}\lesssim\

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}.

To estimate the term with the normal derivative, note that
\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{\nu}u=\partial_{t}^{2}\left(\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right)-2\partial_{t}\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}\partial_{\nu}u-\partial_{t}^{2}\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u
, implying

\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s+2}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s+1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

where the embedding
\iota_{H^{s+2}\rightarrow H^{s+1}}
has been omitted. By Theorem
16
, if
v
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}v-\mathsf{P}v&=&0&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times M,\\
\left(v(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}v(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
v&=&0&&\text{on }\mathbb{R}\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

then
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}u=v
and
\iota_{H^{s+2}\rightarrow H^{s+1}}\partial_{\nu}u=\partial_{\nu}v
. Hence, using Theorem
16
again, one obtains

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s+1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle=\left\|\partial_{\nu}v\right\|_{H^{s+1}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|v(0)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}v(0)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}},

yielding

\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s+2}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}.

To complete the proof, we prove that the remainder terms on the right-hand side can be removed. The embedding

\begin{array}[]{cccc}K:&\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+2}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\\
&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+2}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{1}\right)\end{array}

is compact, by Proposition
12
-
(i)
. We show that the operator

\begin{array}[]{cccc}A:&\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+2}&\longrightarrow&H^{s+2}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\\
&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\end{array}

is one-to-one, using the fact that the operator

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}&\longrightarrow&H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\\
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\end{array}

is one-to-one, by
H^{s}
-observability. Assume that
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
is such that
\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u=0
. As above, one has
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u=v
and
\iota_{H^{s+2}\rightarrow H^{s}}\partial_{\nu}u=\partial_{\nu}v
, where
v
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}v-\mathsf{P}v&=&0&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times M,\\
\left(v(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}v(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
v&=&0&&\text{on }\mathbb{R}\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

Since
\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}v=0
in
H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
H^{s}
-observability gives
\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}u^{1}\right)=0
. Thus, one finds
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)=0
, and
H^{s+2}
-observability is a consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 30
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
be Hilbert spaces. Consider two continuous linear operators
A:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
K:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
. Assume that
K
is compact and that there exists
C>0
such that

\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\left(\left\|Ax\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}}+\left\|Kx\right\|_{\mathcal{Z}}\right),\quad x\in\mathcal{X}.

Then the kernel of
A
is finite-dimensional. If moreover
A
is one-to-one, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, one has

\|x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq C^{\prime}\left\|Ax\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}},\quad x\in\mathcal{X}.

The proof is straightforward, and is omitted. Here,
A
