is one-to-one: the information from Lemma
30
about the kernel is used below.

3.2
Decreasing the level of regularity

Consider
\Theta^{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and assume that
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta^{1}
holds. As the level of regularity can be increased, by the part of the proof of Section 3.1, we may assume that
s\geq 1
, without loss of generality. We prove that for
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
H^{s-2r}
-observability for
\Theta^{2}
holds, for all
\Theta^{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
\pi_{k}\Theta^{2}\neq 0
on
\supp\pi_{k}\Theta^{1}
, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. Consider
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}
, and denote by
u
the associated solution.

Following the proof of Section 3.1, one might be inclined to define
\tilde{u}(t)=\mathsf{P}^{-r}u(t)
. However, this is not always possible, for example if
\mathsf{P}=\Delta+\lambda
, with
\lambda
in the spectrum of the Dirichlet Laplacian. To overcome this difficulty, we use the shift operator of Proposition
12
-
(iii)
. We introduce
\left(\tilde{u}^{0},\tilde{u}^{1}\right)
as the unique element of
\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
such that
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}\tilde{u}^{0},\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r}^{r}\tilde{u}^{1}\right)
, and set
\tilde{u}
as the solution associated with
\left(\tilde{u}^{0},\tilde{u}^{1}\right)
.

By
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta^{1}
, one has

\left\|\tilde{u}^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}+\left\|\tilde{u}^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta^{1})\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r}^{r}
are continuous, one has
\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}\lesssim\left\|\tilde{u}^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}+\left\|\tilde{u}^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}
, implying

\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta^{1})\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Now, Theorem
20
gives

\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta^{1})\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|\tilde{u}^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\tilde{u}^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}.

Note that embeddings in the remainder terms are omitted here, as
s\geq 1
(see Remark
21
). Using Proposition
12
-
(iii)
, one finds

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{u}^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\tilde{u}^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}=\

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+\frac{1}{2}}^{r}\right)^{-1}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r-\frac{1}{2}}^{r}\right)^{-1}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}.

With the continuity of
\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+\frac{1}{2}}^{r}\right)^{-1}
and
\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r-\frac{1}{2}}^{r}\right)^{-1}
, one obtains

\left\|\tilde{u}^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\tilde{u}^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}},

implying

\displaystyle\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}\lesssim\

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta^{1})\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}.

Next, we want to replace
\left\|\diag(\Theta^{1})\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
by
\left\|\diag(\Theta^{1})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}
, up to a remainder term. The idea is the following: if
\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r}^{r}=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}^{r}
, as in the case
\mathsf{P}=\Delta
for example, then we can prove that
\partial_{t}^{2r}\tilde{u}=u
. In the general case, one has the following lemma.

Lemma 31
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
\left(v^{0},v^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
, and
v
the solution associated with
\left(v^{0},v^{1}\right)
, one has

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}v\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}v\right)\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}v^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}v^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}.

A proof of Lemma
31
is given below. Arguing as above, one finds

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\tilde{u}^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\tilde{u}^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-1}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}.

As
\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}\tilde{u}=u
, by Corollary
17
, Lemma
31
gives

\displaystyle\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}+

\displaystyle\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\Theta^{1})\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}u^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}u^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}.

(28)

Note that (
28
) holds true if
\Theta^{1}
is replaced by some
\Theta^{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
\pi_{k}\Theta^{2}\neq 0
on
\supp\pi_{k}\Theta^{1}
, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. To complete the proof, we show that the remainder terms on the right-hand side of (
28
) can be removed, when
\Theta^{1}
is replaced by such
\Theta^{2}
. The embedding

\begin{array}[]{cccc}K:&\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+\frac{1}{2}}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-\frac{1}{2}}}u^{1}\right)\end{array}

is compact, by Proposition
12
-
(i)
. For
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, introduce

\begin{array}[]{cccc}A_{\Theta,s^{\prime}}:&\mathcal{K}^{s^{\prime}+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s^{\prime}}&\longrightarrow&H^{s^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\\
&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u\end{array}.

By (
28
) and Lemma
30
, the kernel of
A_{\Theta^{1},s-2r}
is finite-dimensional. Note also that
H^{s}
-observability for
\Theta^{1}
implies that
A_{\Theta^{1},s}
is one-to-one.

Lemma 32
.

Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Theta^{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
A_{\Theta^{1},s}
is one-to-one, and such that for all
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, the kernel of
A_{\Theta^{1},s-2r}
is finite-dimensional. Then, for
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
\Theta^{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
\pi_{k}\Theta^{2}\neq 0
on
\supp\pi_{k}\Theta^{1}
, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,
A_{\Theta^{2},s-2r}
is one-to-one.

A proof of Lemma
32
is given below. Now, using (
28
) with
\Theta^{2}
instead of
\Theta^{1}
, and Lemma
30
again, one concludes that
H^{s-2r}
-observability for
\Theta^{2}
holds. As explained in the beginning of Section 3, this completes the proof of Theorem
4
.

Now, we prove Lemma
31
and Lemma
32
.

Proof of Lemma
31
.

By interpolation, one may assume that
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Theorem
16
, one has
\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}v=\partial_{\nu}\partial_{t}^{2r}v=\partial_{\nu}\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r+1}^{r}v
. The triangular inequality gives

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{t}^{2r}\partial_{\nu}v\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}v\right)\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\|\partial_{\nu}\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r+1}^{r}v-\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}v\right)\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Set
w=\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r+1}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}\right)v
. By Theorem
16
and Corollary
17
,
w
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}w-\mathsf{P}w&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(w(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}w(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(w^{0},w^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
w&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

where
\left(w^{0},w^{1}\right)=\left(\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r+1}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{r}\right)v^{0},\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r}^{r}\right)v^{1}\right)
. Hence, using Theorem
16
and Proposition
12
-
(iii)
, one obtains

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\nu}w\right\|_{H^{s-2r}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|w^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}+\left\|w^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}v^{0}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}v^{1}\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}},

and this gives the desired result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
32
.

For
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, denote by
N_{\Theta,s^{\prime}}
the kernel of
A_{\Theta,s^{\prime}}
, that is,

N_{\Theta,s^{\prime}}=\left\{\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s^{\prime}+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s^{\prime}},\diag(\Theta)\partial_{\nu}u=0\right\}.

Note that by definition, one has

N_{\Theta,s^{\prime}}\subset N_{\tilde{\Theta},s^{\prime}},\quad s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},

(29)

if
\pi_{k}\Theta\neq 0
on
\supp\pi_{k}\tilde{\Theta}
, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, and for
s_{1}>s_{2}
, the map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Phi_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\Theta):&N_{\Theta,s_{1}}&\longrightarrow&N_{\Theta,s_{2}}\\
&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s_{1}+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s_{2}+1}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s_{2}}}u^{1}\right)\end{array}

is well-defined, injective, and compact.

Consider
\Theta^{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
\pi_{k}\Theta^{2}\neq 0
on
\supp\pi_{k}\Theta^{1}
, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. We claim that
N_{\Theta^{2},s-2r}
is finite-dimensional for all
r\geq 0
. Indeed, for
r\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from the assumptions of Lemma
32
and (
29
). For
r\geq 0
, as
\Phi_{s-2r,s-\lfloor 2r\rfloor-1}(\Theta^{2})
is one-to-one,
N_{\Theta^{2},s-2r}
is isomorphic to a subspace of
N_{\Theta^{2},s-\lfloor 2r\rfloor-1}
, and hence, is finite-dimensional.

Consider
r\geq 0
. We prove that

\Phi_{s-2r,s-2r-1}(\Theta^{2})\text{ is an isomorphism.}

(30)

It suffices to show that
\Phi_{s-2r,s-2r-1}(\Theta^{2})
is onto. Consider
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in N_{\Theta^{2},s-2r-1}
, and write
U(t)=(u(t),\partial_{t}u(t))
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, where
u
is the solution associated with
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
. As the distance between
\supp\pi_{k}\Theta^{1}
and
(\supp\pi_{k}\Theta^{2})^{\complement}
is positive for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for
t\in[0,\varepsilon)
,
U(t)\in N_{\Theta^{1},s-2r-1}
.

For
t\in(0,\varepsilon)
, set
V_{t}=\frac{1}{t}(U(t)-U(0))\in N_{\Theta^{1},s-2r-1}
. One has

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}\left(\frac{1}{t}(u(t)-u^{0})\right)\underset{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\longrightarrow}\partial_{t}u(0)=u^{1}\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2r},

(31)

and

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-1}}\left(\frac{1}{t}(\partial_{t}u(t)-u^{1})\right)\underset{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\longrightarrow}\partial_{t}^{2}u(0)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-2r}u^{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-1}.

(32)

As
N_{\Theta^{1},s-2r-1}
is finite-dimensional, the norm of
\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}
is equivalent to the norm

\mathcal{N}\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)=\left\|\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-1}}u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-1}}.

By (
31
) and (
32
),
(V_{t})_{t>0}
is a Cauchy sequence for the norm
\mathcal{N}
, and thus, it converges in
N_{\Theta^{1},s-2r}
. Write
\left(v^{0},v^{1}\right)
for its limit. Using (
31
) and (
32
) again, one finds

\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}v^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r-1}}v^{1}\right)=\left(u^{1},\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-2r}u^{0}\right).

By Proposition
12
-
(ii)
, there exists
\tilde{u}^{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+2}
such that
u^{0}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}\tilde{u}^{0}
and
v^{1}=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-2r+1}\tilde{u}^{0}
. One has

\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}\tilde{u}^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r}}v^{0}\right)=\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right).

This gives
\Phi_{s-2r,s-2r-1}(\Theta^{2})\left(\tilde{u}^{0},v^{0}\right)=\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
, if we show that
\left(\tilde{u}^{0},v^{0}\right)\in N_{\Theta^{2},s-2r-1}
. If
\tilde{u}
is the solution associated with
\left(\tilde{u}^{0},v^{0}\right)
, then by Theorem
16
, one has
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2r+1}}\tilde{u}=u
and

\iota_{H^{s-2r+1}\rightarrow H^{s-2r}}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{u}=\partial_{\nu}u.

As
\diag(\Theta^{2})\partial_{\nu}u=0\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, this implies
\left(\tilde{u}^{0},v^{0}\right)\in N_{\Theta^{2},s-2r-1}
, completing the proof of (
30
).

By iteration, one obtains an isomorphism between
N_{\Theta^{2},s}
and
N_{\Theta^{2},s-2r}
for
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. As
N_{\Theta^{1},s}=\{0\}
, (
29
) gives
N_{\Theta^{2},s}=\{0\}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
32
.
∎

Appendix A
The case of internal observability

Here, we explain how to adapt the methods of this article to the case of internal observability. Consider
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Definition 33

(
\mathcal{K}^{s}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T\right)
)
.

We say that
\mathcal{K}^{s}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T\right)
holds if there exists
C>0
such that for all
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
,

\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\leq C\left\|\diag(\chi)u\right\|_{L^{2}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s})},

where
u
is the solution of (
1
) with initial data
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
.

Note that the multiplication operator
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s}\mapsto\diag(\chi)u\in\mathcal{K}^{s}
is well-defined, and commutes with the embeddings of Proposition
12
. As in the boundary case, one can prove that
\mathcal{K}^{s}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T\right)
is equivalent with a controllability property, for the equation (
1
) with a source term of the form
\diag(\chi)F
, with
F\in L^{2}((0,T),\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s})
. One can check that the solution of such a system is well-defined, by adapting the proof of Theorem
16
. The analogue of Theorem
4
is the following result.

Theorem 34
.

Consider
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. If
s_{1}<s_{2}
, then for all
T>0
,
\mathcal{K}^{s_{1}}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T\right)
implies
\mathcal{K}^{s_{2}}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T\right)
. If
s_{1}>s_{2}
, then for all
0<T_{1}<T_{2}
,
\mathcal{K}^{s_{1}}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T_{1}\right)
implies
\mathcal{K}^{s_{2}}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T_{2}\right)
.

The proof of Theorem
34
is simpler than that of Theorem
4
, so we only sketch it. To increase the regularity level, one uses the following lemma.

Lemma 35
.

Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. There exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\left[\diag(\chi),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}\right]u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}\leq C\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}},\quad u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}.

Sketch of proof of Lemma
35
.

By interpolation, it suffices to prove Lemma
35
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. For
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
, one has

\left\|\left[\diag(\chi),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}\right]u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}=\left\|\left[\diag(\chi),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}\right]u\right\|_{H^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s+1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}},

and the same holds for
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
. Now, consider
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s\leq-1
, and
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
. Note that

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\diag(\chi),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}\right]u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\left|\left\langle\left[\diag(\chi),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}\right]u,\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}\right|,v\in\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1},\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}v\right\|_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}\leq 1\right\},

as
\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}
has a dense range. For
v\in\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}
, using the case
s\in\mathbb{N}
, one finds

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\left[\diag(\chi),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}\right]u,\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\langle\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u,\left[\diag(\chi),\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}^{\ast}\right]v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-1}}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}v\right\|_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}},

and that completes the proof of Lemma
35
.
∎

Lemma
35
and
\mathcal{K}^{s}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T\right)
yield

\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\chi)u\right\|_{L^{2}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s+2})}+\left\|\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}},

for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
. The remainder term is compact, and
\mathcal{K}^{s}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T\right)
implies that the operator
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\mapsto\diag(\chi)u
is one-to-one. This proves that
\mathcal{K}^{s}
-observability implies
\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
-observability.

To decrease the regularity level, one relies on the following result about the shift operator of Proposition
12
. We use the notation
\mathcal{S}_{s}^{-1}=\left(\mathcal{S}_{s}^{1}\right)^{-1}
, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 36
.

Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. There exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\left[\diag(\chi),\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}\right]u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}\leq C\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-3}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-3}},\quad u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2}.

Sketch of proof of Lemma
36
.

Using Proposition
12
and Lemma
35
, one finds

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\diag(\chi),\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}\right]u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{1}\left(\diag(\chi)\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}u\right)-\diag(\chi)\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{1}\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}u\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{1}\left(\diag(\chi)\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}u\right)-\diag(\chi)\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{1}\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}\left(\diag(\chi)\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}u\right)-\diag(\chi)\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\mathcal{S}_{s-1}^{-1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-3}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-3}},

for all
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2}
.
∎

Assume that
\mathcal{K}^{s}
-observability for
\left(\chi,T_{1}\right)
holds. Then, Lemma
36
gives

\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-3}}\lesssim\left\|\diag(\chi)u\right\|_{L^{2}((0,T_{1}),\mathcal{K}^{s-2})}+\left\|\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-3}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-3}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-4}}u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-3}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-4}},

for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s-2}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-3}
. For
T>0
and
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, set

\begin{array}[]{cccc}A_{T,s^{\prime}}:&\mathcal{K}^{s^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{K}^{s^{\prime}-1}&\longrightarrow&L^{2}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s^{\prime}})\\
&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\diag(\chi)u\end{array},

and write
\Ker A_{T,s^{\prime}}
for the kernel of that operator. Then
A_{T_{1},s}
is one-to-one,
\Ker A_{T_{1},s-2}
is finite-dimensional, and to complete the proof of Theorem
34
, it suffices to prove that
A_{T_{2},s-2}
is one-to-one, for all
T_{2}>T_{1}
. For
s_{1}>s_{2}
and
T>0
, introduce the embedding

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Phi_{T,s_{1},s_{2}}:&\Ker A_{T,s_{1}}&\longrightarrow&\Ker A_{T,s_{2}}\\
&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&\longmapsto&\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s_{2}}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s_{1}-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s_{2}-1}}u^{1}\right)\end{array}.

Consider
T_{2}>T_{1}
and
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
. We prove that
\Phi_{T_{2},s-\sigma,s-\sigma-1}
is an isomorphism. Take
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\Ker A_{T_{2},s-\sigma-1}
. For
t\in(0,T_{2}]
, set

V_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\left(\left(u(t),\partial_{t}u(t)\right)-\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right).

As
T_{2}>T_{1}
, one has
V_{t}\in\Ker A_{T_{1},s-\sigma-1}
for all
t>0
sufficiently small. In addition,
V_{t}
converges to a limit as
t\rightarrow 0^{+}
, for one particular norm on
\Ker A_{T_{1},s-\sigma-1}
, and hence for any norm on
\Ker A_{T_{1},s-\sigma-1}
, as
\Ker A_{T_{1},s-\sigma-1}
is finite-dimensional. This gives
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\Phi_{T_{2},s-\sigma,s-\sigma-1}\left(\Ker A_{T_{2},s-\sigma}\right)
. Hence,
\Phi_{T_{2},s-\sigma,s-\sigma-1}
is an isomorphism. In particular,
\Ker A_{T_{2},s-2}
is isomorphic to
\Ker A_{T_{2},s}=\{0\}
, and this completes the proof of Theorem
34
.

Appendix B
Proof of the results of Section 1

B.1
Proof of Proposition
12

We start by giving some details about
(i)
. If
s\geq 0
then the map
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}:\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}
is just a natural inclusion, and is thus one-to-one. It is an embedding, and it will often be omitted. If
s+\delta<0
, then by definition
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}
is the restriction operator

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{K}^{s}\\
u&\longmapsto&u_{|\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}\end{array}.

In Step 5 below, we prove that
\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-\delta}}
has dense range, implying that
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}
is one-to-one if
s+\delta<0
. By definition, if
s+\delta\geq 0>s
, one has

\left\langle\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}(u),v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}}=\left\langle u,\overline{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

for
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}
and
v\in\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}=\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{|s|}
. As
\mathscr{D}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}
, one sees that
\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s}
is dense in
L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. This implies that
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}
is one-to-one in the case
s+\delta\geq 0>s
.

To prove
(iii)
, one can always assume that
r=1
: the operator
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r}
is then defined by

\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r}:\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r-1}^{1}}\mathcal{K}^{s+r-2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r-3}^{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+3}^{1}}\mathcal{K}^{s-r+2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+1}^{1}}\mathcal{K}^{s-r}.

Note that in particular, (
7
) holds true by definition.

The proof is organized as follows. First, we prove
(ii)
for
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\geq r
, except the inequality. Second, we construct the shift operator in three steps. Third, using the shift operator, we prove
(i)
. Finally, we complete the proof of
(ii)
and
(iii)
. We often assume that
s\in\mathbb{Z}
: the case
s\in\mathbb{R}
follows by interpolation.

Step 1: Proof of
(ii)
for
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\geq r
(except the inequality).

As
s-r\geq 0
here, we know that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}u=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{r}u
for all
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}
. Hence, all equations of this step can be understood in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
(or in
H^{-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
), and we omit embeddings.

We prove by induction on
r\in\mathbb{N}
that for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\geq r
, and all
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}
, if
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
then
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
. It is true for
r=0
, as
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{0}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}
. Take
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that the result holds. Fix
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\geq r+1
, and
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
such that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r+1}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}
. We want to show that
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}
. By induction, we only need to prove that
v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1-r}
.

We start by proving that
v\in H^{s+1-r}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. By definition of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}
, we know that
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
. One has
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r-1}v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r+1}u
, so that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r-1}v\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}
by assumption. In particular, one has
v\in H^{s-r}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r-1}v\in H^{s-r-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
: this gives

\Delta v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r-1}v+\left(X+q\right)v\in H^{s-r-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}),

with equality in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. As
s-r\geq 1
, one also has
v\in H^{1}_{0}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Thus, by a standard elliptic regularity result, applied componentwise, one finds
v\in H^{s+1-r}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Now, we prove that
v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1-r}
. Assume that
s-r
is odd and write
s-r=2\sigma+1
. By definition, the fact that
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
gives
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for
k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma+r\rrbracket
. As
v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{r}u
, this implies

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}v\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for }k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma\rrbracket,

yielding
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1-r}
. Now, assume that
s-r
is even and write
s-r=2\sigma
. By definition,
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
gives
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for
k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma+r-1\rrbracket
, implying

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{k}v\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for }k\in\llbracket 0,\sigma-1\rrbracket.

As
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r-1}v=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r+1}u\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}
, one also has
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{\sigma}v\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, so that
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1-r}
.

Step 2: Injectivity of the shift operator for
s\in\mathbb{N}
.

Consider
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. We show that for
|\mu|
sufficiently large and for
s\in\mathbb{N}
, the operator

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu:&\mathcal{K}^{s+1}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{K}^{s-1}\\
&u&\longmapsto&\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu\right)u\end{array}

is one-to-one, where the embedding
\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
has been omitted (as explained in the beginning of the proof, this embedding is indeed an embedding even if
s=0
). Fix
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
u=(u^{1},\cdots,u^{N})\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
such that
\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu\right)u=0
. Write
(\pi_{1},\cdots,\pi_{N})
for the projections associated with the canonical basis of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. If
s=0
, one has

\mathsf{P}_{\!0}u=-i\mu u\in\mathcal{K}^{1}\subset L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}),

so that Step 1 gives
u\in\mathcal{K}^{2}
. Hence,
u\in\mathcal{K}^{2}
for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
.

For
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, one has

-\Delta u^{k}+\pi_{k}\left(Xu+qu\right)+i\mu u^{k}=0.

Multiplication by
\overline{u^{k}}
, integration on
M
and an integration by parts give

\int_{M}\left(\left|\nabla u^{k}\right|^{2}+i\mu|u^{k}|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{M}\overline{u^{k}}\pi_{k}\left(Xu+qu\right)\mathrm{d}x.

(33)

Computing the real part and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields

\int_{M}\left|\nabla u^{k}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\leq\left\|Xu+qu\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

By the Poincaré inequality, one obtains

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Thus, there exists
C>0
depending only on
X
and
q
such that

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

(34)

Now, compute the imaginary part of (
33
) and use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to find

|\mu|\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\leq\left\|Xu+qu\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Together with (
34
), this gives

|\mu|\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

with
C>0
depending only on
X
and
q
. Thus, for
|\mu|
chosen sufficiently large, one has
u=0
.

Step 3: Surjectivity of the shift operator for
s=0
.

We prove that

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathsf{P}_{\!0}+i\mu:&H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})&\longrightarrow&H^{-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\\
&u&\longmapsto&\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!0}+i\mu\right)u\end{array}

is onto. By definition of the operator
\mathsf{P}_{\!0}:H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, we need to show that for
v\in H^{-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, there exists
u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that

-\left\langle\nabla u,\nabla\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\langle i\mu u-(X+q)u,\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\left\langle v,\overline{\phi}\right\rangle_{H^{-1},H_{0}^{1}},\quad\phi\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}),

where one has used the notation

\left\langle\nabla u,\nabla\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left\langle\nabla u^{k},\nabla\phi^{k}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}.

Using the Lax-Milgram theorem, it suffices to prove a coercivity inequality of the form

\left|-\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left\langle i\mu u-(X+q)u,u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|\gtrsim\|u\|_{H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2},\quad u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(35)

Take
u\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Using the triangular inequality, one has

\begin{split}&\left|-\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left\langle i\mu u-(X+q)u,u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|\\
\geq&\left|-\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+i\mu\|u\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\right|-\left|\left\langle(X+q)u,u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|.\end{split}

(36)

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz and Poincaré inequalities, one obtains

\left|\left\langle(X+q)u,u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

For
\varepsilon>0
, one writes

\left|\left\langle(X+q)u,u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|\leq c_{1}\left(\varepsilon\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\right).

For the other term of (
36
), simply write

\left|-\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+i\mu\|u\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\right|\geq c_{2}\left(\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+|\mu|\|u\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\right).

Thus, coming back to (
36
), one obtains

\displaystyle\left|-\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left\langle i\mu u-(X+q)u,u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle\left(c_{2}-c_{1}\varepsilon\right)\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left(c_{2}|\mu|-\frac{c_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)\|u\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}.

We choose
\varepsilon
so that
c_{2}-c_{1}\varepsilon>0
. Then, for
|\mu|
sufficiently large, the coercivity inequality (
35
) holds.

Step 4: Construction of the shift operator.

We start by the case
s\in\mathbb{N}
. As above, we omit the embeddings. We proceed by induction on
s\in\mathbb{N}
and prove that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
is onto. By Step 3, it holds for
s=0
. Assume that it holds for some fixed
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Take
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s}
. We show that there exists
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
such that
v=\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}+i\mu\right)u
.

As
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s}\subset\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
, there exists
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
such that
v=\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu\right)u
. We apply Step 1. One has
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
,
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s}
, and
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u=v-i\mu u\in\mathcal{K}^{s}
. As
s\geq 0
, this gives
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}
.

Together with Step 2, this shows that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
is an isomorphism for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that as
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}
are of the same form, the operator
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}-i\mu:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s}
is also an isomorphism for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
. Now, for
s\leq-1
, we define
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
as the adjoint of
\mathsf{P}^{\ast}-i\mu:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-1}
: it is an isomorphism. Note that for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
is a continuous isomorphism between Hilbert spaces, so its inverse is continuous. As
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}
is now defined for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can define
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
by interpolation.

Step 5: Proof of
(i)
.

Note that at this stage, the injectivity of the operator
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}
is yet to be proven if
s+\delta<0
. To that purpose, the commutativity property (
8
) is needed.

We start by proving (
5
). It suffices to prove it for
r=1
, that is

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+\delta}:\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}.

(37)

By interpolation, it suffices to prove it for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. If
s\geq 0
, then (
37
) is true as it holds in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Similarly, one has

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{\ast}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+1}}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1}}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+\delta}^{\ast}:\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{s-1},\quad s\geq 0.

(38)

Computing the adjoint of (
38
) and using (
4
) and (
10
), one finds

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{-\tilde{s}-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-\tilde{s}-1-\delta}}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-\tilde{s}}=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-\tilde{s}-\delta}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{-\tilde{s}+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-\tilde{s}+1-\delta}},\quad\tilde{s}\geq 0,

and this gives (
37
) for
s=-\tilde{s}-\delta
, that is, in the case
s\leq-1
and
s+\delta\leq 0
. Thus, (
37
) only remains to be proven for
s\leq-1
and
s+\delta\geq 1
. In this case, for
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\subset\mathcal{K}^{2}
and
v\in\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{1-s}\subset\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{2}
, one writes

\left\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u,v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{1-s}}=\left\langle\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u,\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}^{\ast}v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{-s-1}}=\left\langle u,\overline{\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}^{\ast}v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

as
s+1+\delta\geq 0\geq s+1
. Using
-s\geq 0
and
s+\delta\geq 0
, one has

\left\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u,v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{1-s}}=\left\langle u,\overline{\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}^{\ast}v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\left\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+\delta}u,\overline{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

As
s-1+\delta\geq 0\geq s-1
, one finds

\left\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u,v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{1-s}}=\left\langle\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+\delta}u,v\right\rangle_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1},\mathcal{K}_{\ast}^{1-s}},

and this gives (
37
).

Now, we prove that it implies the commutativity property (
8
). The case
r=1
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
suffices, that is,

\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\delta}^{1}.

(39)

For
s\geq 0
, one has
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}
by definition, yielding (
39
) as a direct consequence of (
37
). For
s\leq-1
, one has

\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}=\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!-s}^{\ast}-i\mu\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{-s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{-s-1}}\right)^{\ast},

implying
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}+i\mu\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}
by (
4
) and (
10
). Hence, (
39
) is also a consequence of (
37
) in that case.

Now, we complete the proof of
(i)
. As
\mathscr{D}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\mathcal{K}^{1+\delta}
,
\mathcal{K}^{1+\delta}
is dense in
\mathcal{K}^{1}
. Take
s\in\mathbb{N}
. If
s=2\sigma
, then using (
8
), one can factor the map
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}
into

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}:\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}_{\!\delta+\sigma}^{\sigma}}\mathcal{K}^{\delta}\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{0}}}\mathcal{K}^{0}\xrightarrow{\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}}\mathcal{K}^{s}

(40)

and this proves the density of
\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}
in
\mathcal{K}^{s}
. Similarly, if
s=2\sigma+1
then one writes

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}:\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{S}_{1+\delta+\sigma}^{\sigma}}\mathcal{K}^{1+\delta}\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{1}}}\mathcal{K}^{1}\xrightarrow{\left(\mathcal{S}_{1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}}\mathcal{K}^{s}.

(41)

This proves that all the maps of
(i)
are embeddings. The rest of the proof of
(i)
is similar, using (
40
), (
41
). For the compactness property, note that for
0<\delta<\frac{1}{2}
, one has
\mathcal{K}^{\delta}=H^{\delta}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, implying the compactness of
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{0}}
by the Rellich theorem.

Step 6: End of the proof of
(ii)
.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
such that
s\leq r-1
, the case
s\geq r
having been carried out in Step 1. Take
w\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}
and
v\in\mathcal{K}^{s-r}
such that

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}w=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}v.

(42)

We seek
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
such that

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}u=w

(43)

and

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u=v.

(44)

Note that (
43
) implies

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}u-v\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}u-\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}v

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{r}w-\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}v=0

and this gives (
44
) since
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}
is one-to-one. Hence, it suffices to find
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2r}
such that (
43
) holds. Note that such a
u
is unique since
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+2r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+2r-1}}
is one-to-one. If
s+r\geq 1
, the embedding could be omitted. If however
s+r\leq 0
, (
43
) means that
u
is an extension of
w
as a continuous linear form.

Take
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s-r-1+2\sigma\geq 0
. Applying
\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r-1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}
to (
42
), one finds

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+2\sigma-1}^{r}\circ\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r+\sigma-1}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}w=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r+2\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r+2\sigma-1}}\circ\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}v.

Apply Step 1 with
W=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r+\sigma-1}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}w\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r+2\sigma-1}
and
\tilde{s}=s+2\sigma\geq r
: there exists
U\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r+2\sigma}
such that

W=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+2\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r+2\sigma-1}}U.

Hence, one finds

w=\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r+\sigma-1}^{\sigma}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+2\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r+2\sigma-1}}U=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r+\sigma}^{\sigma}U,

and this is (
43
) with
u=\mathcal{S}_{\!s+r+\sigma}^{\sigma}U
.

We now prove (
6
) by induction on
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
. We start with the case
r=1
. Take
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
, and write

\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}=\left\|\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}\right)^{-1}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\lesssim\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}.

Using the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}
and the triangular inequality, one obtains

\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\lesssim\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}},

a better estimate than (
6
) in the case
r=1
, since one has

\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}=\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}.

Now, we take
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
such that (
6
) holds, and we prove that (
6
) also holds for
r+1
. Take
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}
. We want to show that

\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}}\lesssim\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r+1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}.

By induction, one has

\|u\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}}\lesssim\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1-r}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}.

The case
r=1
gives

\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1-r}}\lesssim\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}.

Using the definition of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r+1}
and the commutativity property (
5
), one finds

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1-r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r+1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}+\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r+1}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}.

This completes the proof of
(ii)
.

Step 7: End of the proof of
(iii)
.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{R}
. We prove (
9
), that is,

\left\|\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r}\right)u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r}}\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}},

for
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r}
. Note that this is true for
r=1
: one has

\left\|\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{1}\right)u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}=\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}},

for all
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
. Now, assume that the result is true for some
r\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, and take
u\in\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}
. We write

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r+1}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r+1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s-r}^{1}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1}^{r}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}-\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}+i\mu\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}\right)\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1}^{r}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}\circ\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1}^{r}\right)-i\mu\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1}^{r}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}\circ\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1}^{r}\right)-i\mu\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1}^{r}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}

and we use the continuity of
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-r}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1}^{r}
to find

\left\|\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r+1}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r+1}\right)u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}\lesssim\left\|\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+1}^{r}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1}^{r}\right)u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r+1}}+\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r-1}}.

By induction, we get

\left\|\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}^{r+1}-\mathcal{S}_{\!s}^{r+1}\right)u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-r-1}}\lesssim\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+r}}u\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+r}},

and this completes the proof.

B.2
Solutions of the wave equations

B.2.1
Proof of Theorem
16

The proof of Theorem
16
is organized as follows. First, we check that the assumptions of the Hille-Yosida theorem are fulfilled, to construct the solution for
s\geq 0
, with the regularity result

u\in\bigcap_{k=0}^{s+1}\mathscr{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1-k}).

Second, we construct the solution for
s<0
by using the shift operator of Proposition
12
-
(iii)
. Third, we prove Theorem
16
-
(ii)
and the regularity result

u\in\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1-k}),\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

Fourth, we construct the solution of the wave equation with a source term as is Theorem
16
-
(iv)
. Finally, we prove the results about the normal derivative. Note that by interpolation, we can always assume that
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Step 1: Construction of the solution for
s\geq 0
with the Hille-Yosida theorem.

We write
\mathcal{X}
for the Hilbert space
\mathcal{K}^{1}\times\mathcal{K}^{0}
and
A:\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
for the unbounded operator

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{Id}\\
-\mathsf{P}_{\!1}&0\end{pmatrix}

with domain
D(A)=\mathcal{K}^{2}\times\mathcal{K}^{1}
. We also write
U=\binom{u}{\partial_{t}u}
so that the wave equation reads (formally at first)

\partial_{t}U+AU=0\quad\text{ with }\quad U(0)=\binom{u^{0}}{u^{1}}.

Lemma 37

(The iterated domains of
A
)
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, one has

D(A^{k})=\mathcal{K}^{k+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{k}.

(45)

Proof.

By definition, one has
D(A^{0})=\mathcal{K}^{1}\times\mathcal{K}^{0},\quad D(A^{1})=\mathcal{K}^{2}\times\mathcal{K}^{1}
, and

D(A^{k+1})=\left\{U\in D(A^{k}),A^{k}U\in D(A)\right\},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}.

Note that we can omit the embeddings here, as we are only working with subspaces of
L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that (
45
) is true. The fact that

\mathcal{K}^{k+2}\times\mathcal{K}^{k+1}\subset D(A^{k+1})

follows from the definitions. Take
U=\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in D(A^{k+1})
. One has
U\in D(A^{k})
and
AU\in D(A^{k})
. This gives
u^{1}\in\mathcal{K}^{k+1}
,
u^{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{k+1}
and
\mathsf{P}u^{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{k}
. By Proposition
12
-
(ii)
, one finds
u^{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{k+2}
and so
U\in\mathcal{K}^{k+2}\times\mathcal{K}^{k+1}
.
∎

Lemma 38

(Assumptions of the Hille-Yosida theorem)
.

The operator
A
is closed, and
D(A)
is dense in
\mathcal{X}
. There exists
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
such that the resolvent set of
A
contains
(-\infty,-\omega)
and such that for all
\lambda<-\omega
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, one has

\left\|R_{\lambda}(A)^{k}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})}\leq\frac{1}{\left|\omega+\lambda\right|^{k}},

where
R_{\lambda}(A)=\left(\lambda\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{X}}-A\right)^{-1}
. The same is true for the operator
-A
.

The proof of Lemma
38
is given below. Fix
s\in\mathbb{N}
and

\binom{u^{0}}{u^{1}}\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}=D(A^{s}).

Using the Hille-Yosida theorem (see for example
[
10
]
, Theorem 3.3.1 and Corollary 2.4.1), together with Lemma
37
and Lemma
38
, one obtains that there exists a unique solution

U\in\bigcap_{l=0}^{s}\mathscr{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R},D(A^{s-l}))=\bigcap_{l=0}^{s}\mathscr{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-l+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s-l})

of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcc}\partial_{t}U+AU&=&0\\
U(0)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\end{array}.\right.

One can check that in fact,
U
is of the form
\left(u,\partial_{t}u\right)
, with

u\in\bigcap_{l=0}^{s+1}\mathscr{C}^{l}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-l+1}).

Note that this gives
u\in H^{s+1}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Proof of Lemma
38
.

The fact that
D(A)
is dense in
\mathcal{X}
is well-known. We prove that
A
is closed. Consider a sequence
\left((u^{0}_{n},u^{1}_{n})\right)_{n}
of elements of
D(A)
such that

\binom{u^{0}_{n}}{u^{1}_{n}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\binom{u^{0}}{u^{1}}\in\mathcal{X}\quad\text{ and }\quad A\binom{u^{0}_{n}}{u^{1}_{n}}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\binom{v^{0}}{v^{1}}\in\mathcal{X}.

By definition one has

u^{1}_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}-v^{0}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{K}^{1}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathsf{P}_{\!1}u^{0}_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}-v^{1}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{K}^{0}.

In particular, this gives
u^{1}=-v^{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{1}
. One also has
u^{0}_{n}\longrightarrow u^{0}
in
\mathcal{K}^{1}
yielding
\mathsf{P}_{\!0}u^{0}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathsf{P}_{\!0}u^{0}
in
\mathcal{K}^{-1}
. This implies
\mathsf{P}_{\!0}u^{0}=-v^{1}\in\mathcal{K}^{0}
, so the ellipticity estimate for
\mathsf{P}
(Proposition
12
-
(ii)
) gives
u^{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{2}
. Thus,
A
is closed.

Now, we show that there exists
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
such that if
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
satisfies
|\lambda|+\omega>0
then

\left\|\left(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-A\right)U\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}\geq\left(|\lambda|+\omega\right)\|U\|_{\mathcal{X}}

(46)

for all
U\in D(A)
. Recall that
\mathcal{X}
is a Hilbert space for scalar product associated with the norm

\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\left\|\nabla u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}.

Fix
U=(u^{0},u^{1})\in D(A)
, and write

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-A\right)U\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}=\

\displaystyle|\lambda|^{2}\left\|U\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}+\left\|AU\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}-2\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\lambda U,AU\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle|\lambda|^{2}\left\|U\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}-2\lambda\operatorname{Re}\left\langle U,AU\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}.

(47)

By definition, one has

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\left\langle U,AU\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\nabla u^{0},\nabla u^{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\mathsf{P}_{\!1}u^{0},u^{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\nabla u^{0},\nabla u^{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\left(\Delta-X-q\right)u^{0},u^{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Integrating by parts, one finds

\operatorname{Re}\left\langle U,AU\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}=\operatorname{Re}\left\langle\left(X+q\right)u^{0},u^{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

and using the Cauchy-Schwarz and Poincaré inequalities, this gives

\left|\operatorname{Re}\left\langle U,AU\right\rangle_{\mathcal{X}}\right|\lesssim\left\|\nabla u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\left\|u^{1}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\|U\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

Coming back to (
47
), one finds that there exists
c>0
such that

\left\|\left(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-A\right)U\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\geq\left(|\lambda|^{2}-c|\lambda|\right)\left\|U\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\geq\left(|\lambda|-c\right)^{2}\left\|U\right\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}

for
|\lambda|>c
. This gives (
46
) with
\omega=-c
.

Next, we show that the operator
\lambda\mathrm{Id}-A:D(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is onto if
|\lambda|
is sufficiently large. Fix
(v^{0},v^{1})\in\mathcal{X}
. We seek
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in D(A)
such that

\left(\lambda\mathrm{Id}-A\right)\binom{u^{0}}{u^{1}}=\binom{v^{0}}{v^{1}},

which reads

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}u^{1}&=&-v^{0}+\lambda u^{0}\\
\left(\Delta-X-q\right)u^{0}-\lambda^{2}u^{0}&=&-v^{1}-\lambda v^{0}\end{array}\right..

Using the Lax-Milgram theorem and the ellipticity of
\mathsf{P}
, it follows if the coercivity inequality

\left|\left\|\nabla u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\lambda^{2}\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left\langle(X+q)u^{0},u^{0}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|\gtrsim\|\nabla u^{0}\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}

(48)

is proven. As above, for
\varepsilon>0
, write

\left|\left\langle(X+q)u^{0},u^{0}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|\lesssim\varepsilon\|\nabla u^{0}\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|u^{0}\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2},

and find, for
c_{1}>0
,
c_{2}>0
, by choosing
\varepsilon
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\left|\left\|\nabla u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\lambda^{2}\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left\langle(X+q)u^{0},u^{0}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\

\displaystyle c_{1}\left\|\nabla u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}+\left(\lambda^{2}-c_{2}\right)\left\|u^{0}\right\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\

\displaystyle\|\nabla u^{0}\|_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}

for
|\lambda|
sufficiently large. This gives (
48
).

At this stage, one has proved that there exists
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(-\infty,-\omega)
is contained in the resolvent set of both
A
and
-A
, and for
\lambda
such that
|\lambda|+\omega>0
, one has

\left\|R_{\lambda}(A)\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})}\leq\frac{1}{\left|\omega+|\lambda|\right|}.

This completes the proof of Lemma
38
.
∎

Step 2: Construction of the solution for
s<0
.

The idea of this step is to construct the solution with the shift operator and the solution in
\mathcal{K}^{3}\times\mathcal{K}^{2}
or
\mathcal{K}^{2}\times\mathcal{K}^{1}
, depending of the parity of
s
. Fix
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s<0
. There exist
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
\alpha\in\{1,2\}
such that
s=-2\sigma+\alpha
. Take
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
. Let
\left(\tilde{u}^{0},\tilde{u}^{1}\right)
be the unique element of
\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{\alpha}
such that

\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\tilde{u}^{0},\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\sigma}^{\sigma}\tilde{u}^{1}\right).

Let
\tilde{u}
be the solution associated with
\left(\tilde{u}^{0},\tilde{u}^{1}\right)
defined above. Set
u(t)=\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\tilde{u}(t)
, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We will refer to
u
as the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&0&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }\mathbb{R}\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

We prove that

u\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-1}),

and that
\partial_{t}^{2}u=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u
. In particular, if
s=-1
or
-2
, it implies
u\in H^{s+1}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
for
T>0
, using the embedding

\mathscr{C}^{0}((0,T),H^{-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow H^{-1}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})

for the case
s=-2
.

Continuity.
The continuity of
u
is a consequence of that of the shift operator
\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}
and that of
\tilde{u}
. In addition, for
T>0
, one has

\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s+1})}\lesssim\left\|\tilde{u}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1})}\lesssim\left\|\left(\tilde{u}^{0},\tilde{u}^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}.

First-order time-derivative.
We show that

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\left(\frac{u(t+\varepsilon)-u(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\sigma}^{\sigma}\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(t)\in\mathcal{K}^{s},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

By Proposition
12
, one has

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}=\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\sigma}^{\sigma}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{\alpha}}=\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\sigma}^{\sigma},

where the last embedding can be omitted as it is just an inclusion. Hence, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
, we can write

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\left(\frac{u(t+\varepsilon)-u(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)-\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\sigma}^{\sigma}\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\sigma}^{\sigma}\left(\frac{\tilde{u}(t+\varepsilon)-\tilde{u}(t)}{\varepsilon}-\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(t)\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\frac{\tilde{u}(t+\varepsilon)-\tilde{u}(t)}{\varepsilon}-\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0.

As above, one also has

\left\|\partial_{t}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s})}\lesssim\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{u}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{\alpha})}\lesssim\left\|\left(\tilde{u}^{0},\tilde{u}^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}},\quad T>0.

Second-order time-derivative.
As above, one shows that
u\in\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-1})
, with

\partial_{t}^{2}u(t)=\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{u}(t),\quad t\in\mathbb{R},

and

\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s-1})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}},\quad T>0.

In particular, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, one finds
\partial_{t}^{2}u(t)=\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{u}(t)=\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\alpha}\tilde{u}(t)
, and by Proposition
12
-
(iii)
, this gives

\partial_{t}^{2}u(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\tilde{u}(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u(t).

Step 3: Regularity, uniqueness and approximation.

Here, we prove the uniqueness result of Theorem
16
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, the regularity result
(i)
, and then
(ii)
and the uniqueness result of Theorem
16
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Uniqueness for
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For
s\in\mathbb{N}
, the uniqueness result of Theorem
16
is given by the Hille-Yosida Theorem. Fix
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s<0
, and
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
. Let
v\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-1})
be such that
\partial_{t}^{2}v(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}v(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\left(v(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}v(0,\cdot)\right)=\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
in
M
. As above, let
\sigma\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
\alpha\in\{1,2\}
be such that
s=-2\sigma+\alpha
. For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, define

\tilde{v}(t)=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}v(t).

As in Step 1, one shows that
\tilde{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{\alpha+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{\alpha})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{\alpha-1})
, with
\partial_{t}\tilde{v}(t)=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}\partial_{t}v(t)
and
\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{v}(t)=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}\partial_{t}^{2}v(t)
. By Proposition
12
-
(iii)
, one finds

\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{v}(t)=\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s-1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}v(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\alpha}\circ\left(\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\right)^{-1}v(t)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!\alpha}\tilde{v}(t).

Hence, the functions
\tilde{v}
and
\tilde{u}
(defined in the previous step) satisfy the same wave equation. Using the uniqueness in the case
s\geq 0
, one finds
\tilde{v}=\tilde{u}
, and so

v(t)=\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\tilde{v}(t)=\mathcal{S}_{\!s+1+\sigma}^{\sigma}\tilde{u}(t)=u(t).

The regularity result
(i)
.
Take
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
. We know that

u\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-1}),

and we show that

u\in\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1-k}).

One can prove that

\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-2})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-3}),

and
\partial_{t}^{2}\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\right)=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-2}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u
. For example, to show that
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-2})
, write

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-2}}\left(\frac{\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u(t+\varepsilon)-\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)-\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}\partial_{t}u(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\left(\frac{u(t+\varepsilon)-u(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)-\partial_{t}u(t)\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\left(\frac{u(t+\varepsilon)-u(t)}{\varepsilon}\right)-\partial_{t}u(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}

\displaystyle\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}0.

By uniqueness,
\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u=\partial_{t}^{2}u
is the solution associated with the initial data
\left(\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}u^{0},\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}u^{1}\right)
, implying

u\in\mathscr{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-2})\cap\mathscr{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-3}).

One also has

\left\|\partial_{t}^{3}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s-2})}=\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}\partial_{t}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s-2})}\lesssim\left\|\partial_{t}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}},

and

\left\|\partial_{t}^{4}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s-3})}=\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s-1}^{2}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s-3})}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s+1})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}},

for
T>0
. The result follows by iteration.

Proof of
(ii)
.
Take
s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\delta>0
. For
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}
, if
u
is the solution with initial data
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
, then arguing as above, one has

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s-1}),

with
\partial_{t}\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u\right)=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\partial_{t}u
and

\partial_{t}^{2}\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u\right)=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\partial_{t}^{2}u=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\circ\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s+\delta}u=\mathsf{P}_{\!\!s}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u.

By uniqueness, one finds

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u=\tilde{u},

where
\tilde{u}
is the solution associated with
\left(\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}u^{0},\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}u^{1}\right)
. By interpolation, this is in fact true for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

We prove the approximation result of
(ii)
. Consider
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\delta>0
, and
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
. Let
u
be the solution with initial data
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
. By Proposition
12
-
(i)
, there exists a sequence
\left((\tilde{u}_{k}^{0},\tilde{u}_{k}^{1})\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements of
\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}
such that, writing
u_{k}^{0}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\tilde{u}_{k}^{0}
and
u_{k}^{1}=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s}}\tilde{u}_{k}^{1}
, one has

\left(u_{k}^{0},u_{k}^{1}\right)\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\text{ in }\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}.

Denote
\tilde{u}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+\delta})
and
u_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{s})
the solutions with initial data
\left(\tilde{u}_{k}^{0},\tilde{u}_{k}^{1}\right)
and
\left(u_{k}^{0},u_{k}^{1}\right)
. One has
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\tilde{u}_{k}=u_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and

\sum_{j=0}^{2}\left\|\partial_{t}^{j}(u_{k}-u)\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s+1-j})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u_{k}^{0},u_{k}^{1}\right)-\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

for
T>0
. Hence, one obtains

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1+\delta}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\tilde{u}_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}u,

in
\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s-1})
. In Step 5, we define the normal derivative of a solution, and we show that

\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}.

for all
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
. Hence, we also have

\left\|\partial_{\nu}\left(u_{k}\right)-\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u_{k}^{0},u_{k}^{1}\right)-\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}

and so

\partial_{\nu}\left(u_{k}\right)\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\partial_{\nu}u,

in
H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Uniqueness for
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Lastly, we show that
(ii)
implies the uniqueness result of Theorem
16
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
. If
u
and
v
are two solutions of the wave equation starting from
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
, then using the uniqueness result for
\tilde{s}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
s>\tilde{s}
, one has

\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{\tilde{s}+1}}u=\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{\tilde{s}+1}}v.

This gives
u=v
, as the map
\iota_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{\tilde{s}+1}}
is one-to-one.

Step 4: Study of the Duhamel term.

In this step, we construct the solution of the wave equation with a source term. We define the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&F&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&0&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

(49)

for
F\in L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
. The solution could be constructed with
F\in L^{1}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{s})
instead, but this is of no use for our main results.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
and
F\in\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. For
\tau\in[0,T]
, let
u_{\tau}
be the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u_{\tau}-\mathsf{P}u_{\tau}&=&0&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&(0,F(\tau))&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

As in the classical Duhamel formula, the solution of (
49
) is given by

\Psi(t)=\int_{0}^{t}u_{\tau}(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau,\quad t\in[0,T].

The function
\Psi
is a one-parameter integral, and as
H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\mathcal{K}^{s}
, one has

u_{\tau}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s+1})\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s})\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s-1})

for all
\tau\in[0,T]
. Thus, the following regularity results hold. First, one has
\Psi\in\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s+1})
, with

\displaystyle\left\|\Psi\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s+1})}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|u_{\tau}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\left(u_{\tau}(0),\partial_{t}u_{\tau}(0)\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s})}=\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}([0,T],H^{s})}.

Second, one has
\Psi\in\mathscr{C}^{1}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s})
, with

\partial_{t}\Psi(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}u_{\tau}(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau,\quad t\in[0,T],

and

\left\|\partial_{t}\Psi\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s})}\leq\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\partial_{t}u_{\tau}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s}}\mathrm{d}\tau\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}([0,T],H^{s})}.

As
\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
is dense in
L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, the previous results hold for all
F\in L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Third, one has
\Psi\in\mathscr{C}^{2}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s-1})
, with

\partial_{t}^{2}\Psi(t)=\partial_{t}u_{t}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}^{2}u_{\tau}(t-\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau=F(t)+\mathsf{P}\Psi(t)

for
t\in[0,T]
, and

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\Psi\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s-1})}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|F\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s-1})}+\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}u_{\tau}(t)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s-1}}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{s-1})}+\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}([0,T],H^{s})}.

As
\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
is dense in

L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],H^{s-1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})),

the previous results hold for
F
in the latter space.

The following duality result will be useful later.

Lemma 39
.

For
F_{1},F_{2}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has

\left\langle u,F_{2}\right\rangle_{L^{2}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\left\langle F_{1},v\right\rangle_{L^{2}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\left\langle u^{0},\partial_{t}v(T)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\left\langle u^{1},v(T)\right\rangle_{L^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})},

(50)

where
u
and
v
are the solutions of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&F_{1}&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(T,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(T,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}v-\mathsf{P}^{\ast}v&=&F_{2}&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(v(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}v(0,\cdot)\right)&=&0&&\text{in }M,\\
v&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

For
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}([0,T],\mathcal{K}^{1})
, an integration by parts gives (
50
). Both sides of (
50
) are continuous with respect to the norm of
L^{2}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, (
50
) holds for all
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
in
L^{2}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
by density.
∎

Step 5: Regularity of the normal derivative.

Fix
T>0
. If
s=0
, then the standard scalar proof works without any change (see for example
[
8
]
). In addition, for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{1}\times\mathcal{K}^{0}
and
F\in L^{1}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{0})
, if
u
is the solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\partial_{t}^{2}u-\mathsf{P}u&=&F&&\text{in }(0,T)\times M,\\
\left(u(0,\cdot),\partial_{t}u(0,\cdot)\right)&=&\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)&&\text{in }M,\\
u&=&0&&\text{on }(0,T)\times\partial M,\end{array}\right.

then one has

\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{1}\times\mathcal{K}^{0}}+\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{0})}.

Case
s>0
.
For
s\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
, we prove that

\partial_{\nu}u\in H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})

with

\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}+\left\|F\right\|_{L^{1}((0,T),H^{s})}

(51)

for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}
and
F\in L^{1}((0,T),H_{0}^{s}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, where
u
is the solution of the wave equation with initial data
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)
and with source term
F
.

We start with the case
F=0
. By density it suffices to prove that for
\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\in\mathcal{K}^{s+2}\times\mathcal{K}^{s+1}
, one has

\left\|\partial_{\nu}u\right\|_{H^{s}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}.

Fix
k\in\llbracket 0,s\rrbracket
, and let
L_{1},\cdots,L_{k}
be smooth vector fields on the Riemannian manifold
\partial M
. We prove

L_{1}\cdots L_{k}\partial_{t}^{s-k}\partial_{\nu}u\in L^{2}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(52)

For
j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
, there exists a smooth vector field
\tilde{L}_{j}
on
M
such that
\tilde{L}_{j}=L_{j}
on the boundary. Define

v=\tilde{L}_{1}\cdots\tilde{L}_{k}\partial_{t}^{s-k}u.

Note that
u\in\mathscr{C}^{s-k}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{k+2})
, so that
v\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. As
s
is positive here, we can omit the subscript of
\mathsf{P}
and use the usual differential operator
\mathsf{P}
. We can write

\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\mathsf{P}\right)v=\left[\left(\partial_{t}^{2}-\mathsf{P}\right),\tilde{L}_{1}\cdots\tilde{L}_{k}\partial_{t}^{s-k}\right]u=Ru

where
R
is a differential operator of order
s+1
. As
u\in H^{s+2}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has
Ru\in H^{1}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. We claim that

\left(v(0),\partial_{t}v(0)\right)\in H_{0}^{1}(M)\times L^{2}(M).

(53)

Then, by the standard case, one has

\left\|\partial_{\nu}v\right\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\left(v(0),\partial_{t}v(0)\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{1}\times\mathcal{K}^{0}}+\left\|Ru\right\|_{L^{2}((0,T)\times M,\mathbb{C}^{N})}\lesssim\left\|\left(u^{0},u^{1}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{K}^{s+1}\times\mathcal{K}^{s}}

implying (
52
). Indeed, if
\mathtt{N}
is a smooth vector field on
M
that coincides with the unit normal vector at the boundary, then one has

\partial_{\nu}v=\left(\mathtt{N}v\right)_{|\partial M}=\left(\tilde{L}_{1}\cdots\tilde{L}_{k}\partial_{t}^{s-k}(\mathtt{N}u)\right)_{|\partial M}+\left(R\partial_{t}^{s-k}u\right)_{|\partial M}

where
R
is a time-independent differential operator of order
k-1
. Using
u\in\mathscr{C}^{s-k}((0,T),\mathcal{K}^{k+1})
and the fact that for
j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
, the vector field
\tilde{L}_{j}
is tangent to the boundary, one finds (
52
).

One has
\partial_{t}^{s-k}u\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{K}^{k+2})
, and (
53
) follows if one proves that
w\in\mathcal{K}^{k+2}
implies
\tilde{L}_{1}\cdots\tilde{L}_{k}w\in H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. For
w\in H^{k+1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has

\left(\tilde{L}_{1}\cdots\tilde{L}_{k}w\right)_{|\partial M}=L_{1}\cdots L_{k}\left(w_{|\partial M}\right)\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial M,\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Indeed, it is true if
w\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
and both sides are continuous with respect to the norm of
H^{k+1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Thus, for
w\in H^{k+1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap H_{0}^{1}(M,\mathbb{C}^{N})
, one has
